Hyperbolic O ⁡ ( N ) O(N) linear sigma model and its mean-field limit
Ruoyuan Liu, Shao Liu, and Tadahiro Oh

Abstract
. We study large N N limits of the hyperbolic O ⁡ ( N ) O(N) linear sigma model ( HLSM N \text{HLSM}_{N} ) on the two-dimensional torus 𝕋 2 \mathbb{T}^{2} , namely, a system of N N interacting stochastic damped nonlinear wave equations (SdNLW) with coupled cubic nonlinearities. After establishing (pathwise) global well-posedness of HLSM N \text{HLSM}_{N} and the limiting equation, called the mean-field SdNLW, we first establish convergence of HLSM N \text{HLSM}_{N} to the mean-field SdNLW with general initial data (under a suitable assumption). In particular, for the local-in-time convergence, we obtain a convergence rate of order N − 1 2 + ε N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon} for any ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . We then show that the invariant Gibbs dynamics for HLSM N \text{HLSM}_{N} converges to that for the mean-field SdNLW with a convergence rate of order N − 1 2 + ε N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon} on any large time intervals.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35L71, 60H15, 35R60

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Hyperbolic
O(N)
linear sigma model

In this paper, we consider the following system of

N
interacting
stochastic damped nonlinear wave equations (SdNLW)
with coupled cubic nonlinearities, posed on the two-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}=(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2}
:
1
1

1

In this formal discussion, we ignore the issue of renormalization.

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)u_{N,j}=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}u_{N,k}^{2}u_{N,j}+\sqrt{2}\xi_{j},\qquad j=1,\dots,N,

(1.1)

where
m>0
and
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a family of independent space-time white noises on

\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
Our main goal is to
study the large
N
limit of solutions to (
1.1
)
and show that (under suitable assumptions on initial data), for each fixed
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
a solution
u_{N,j}
to (
1.1
)
converges in probability to a solution
u_{j}
to the following mean-field SdNLW on
\mathbb{T}^{2}

as
N\to\infty
:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)u_{j}=-\mathbb{E}[u_{j}^{2}]\,u_{j}+\sqrt{2}\xi_{j}.

(1.2)

See
Theorems
1.4

and
1.6
below for the main statements.

In quantum field theory, there are natural models where the field
\vec{u}_{N}=(u_{N,1},\dots,u_{N,N}
) takes values in an

N
-dimensional space,
and it is of importance to investigate
their asymptotic behavior as
N\to\infty
.
Such a large
N
limit often
leads to
a substantial
simplification and has been studied for many models.
In
[
60
]
,
Wilson considered the following

N
-component generalization of the
\Phi^{4}_{2}
-measure:

\displaystyle\begin{split}d\rho_{N}=Z_{N}^{-1}&\exp\bigg(-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}|\nabla u_{N,j}|^{2}+\frac{m}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{N,j}^{2}\,dx\\
&\hphantom{XXX}-\frac{1}{4N}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{N,j}^{2}\Big)^{2}dx\bigg)d\vec{u}_{N},\end{split}

(1.3)

known as
the
O(N)
linear sigma model.
Here, the word “linear” refers to the fact that the target space
\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a linear space.
In
[
47
]
, Shen, Smith, Zhu, and Zhu
studied the large
N
limit
of the
O(N)
linear sigma model
\rho_{N}

via the method of stochastic quantization.
Namely, they studied the following
coupled system of stochastic nonlinear heat equations (SNLH) on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)u_{N,j}=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}u_{N,k}^{2}u_{N,j}+\sqrt{2}\xi_{j}.

(1.4)

It is easy to see that
the measure
\rho_{N}

in (
1.3
) is
formally invariant under the dynamics
of (
1.4
)
and, for this reason,
we refer to (
1.4
)
as the parabolic
O(N)
linear sigma model.
In
[
47
]
,
the authors
showed that a solution
u_{N,j}
to (
1.4
) converges
to the following mean-field SNLH:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)u_{j}=-\mathbb{E}[u_{j}^{2}]\,u_{j}+\sqrt{2}\xi_{j}

(under suitable assumptions on initial data).
This dynamical result in turn implied
convergence of the invariant measure
\rho_{N}
in (
1.3
)
to the limiting Gaussian measure
\mu_{1}^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}

(in a suitable sense),
provided that
m>0
is sufficiently large.
Here,

\mu_{1}

denotes the massive
Gaussian free field measure with mass
m>0
:

\displaystyle d\mu_{1}=Z^{-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\|(m-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigg)du.

(1.5)

See
[
47
, Theorem 1.2]
for a precise statement.
In a recent work
[
14
]
,
Delgadino and Smith
extended the latter result on the measure convergence to
arbitrarily small masses
m>0

by making use of
Talagrand’s inequality

[
54
,
44
,
17
]
.
See
[
48
,
49
]

for further results
on the (parabolic)
O(N)
linear sigma model.
See also a nice survey paper

[
46
]

on the subject, including
physical and mathematical backgrounds on the large
N
methods
and
\frac{1}{N}
expansions,
and the references therein.

As in the scalar case (
N=1
)
studied in
[
23
]
,
the SdNLW system (
1.1
) corresponds to the hyperbolic
counterpart of the
parabolic
O(N)
linear sigma model (
1.4
).
In particular,
the SdNLW system (
1.1
) is the so-called canonical stochastic quantization of the
O(N)
linear sigma model
\rho_{N}
in (
1.3
); see
[
45
]
and also Subsection
1.3
below. For this reason, we refer to the SdNLW system (
1.1
) as the
hyperbolic
O(N)
linear sigma model

(
\text{HLSM}_{N}
)
in the following

Starting from the work
[
41
,
21
]
,
there has been a significant progress on the study
of singular stochastic (damped) nonlinear wave equations,
broadly interpreted with stochastic forcing and / or random initial data
in recent years;
see

[
15
,
22
,
36
,
23
,
56
,
32
,
31
,
33
,
6
,
34
,
37
,
35
,
43
,
53
,
9
,
42
,
7
,
61
]
.
Nevertheless, to the best of our knowledge,
there is no well-posedness result on a wave equation with a mean-field nonlinearity,
in particular in the singular setting
(such as the mean-field SdNLW (
1.2
)),
not to mention the large
N
limit (= the mean-field limit) of a system of nonlinear wave equations.
Thus, the current paper presents the first such results on the hyperbolic
O(N)
linear sigma model
(
1.1
) and its mean-field limit (
1.2
).

1.2.
Large
N
limit of the hyperbolic
O(N)
linear sigma model

We now take a closer look at
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.1
) and its (formal) mean-field limit (
1.2
). Given
j\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\Psi_{j}

denote the stochastic convolution, satisfying the following linear stochastic damped wave equation with zero initial data:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)\Psi_{j}=\sqrt{2}\xi_{j}\\
(\Psi_{j},\partial_{t}\Psi_{j})|_{t=0}=(0,0),\end{cases}

(1.6)

where
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

is the family of independent space-time white noises as in (
1.1
).
As seen in
[
21
,
23
]
,

\Psi_{j}(t)
is
almost surely a distribution of regularity
2
2

2

In describing regularities of functions and distributions,
we use
\varepsilon>0
to denote an arbitrarily small constant.

-\varepsilon
and thus we need to introduce renormalization
in considering its powers.

Let
\mathbf{u}_{N}=\{u_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
be a solution to
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.1
).
Proceeding with the first order expansion
[
28
,
5
,
12
]
:

\displaystyle u_{N,j}=\Psi_{j}+v_{N,j},\qquad j=1,\dots,N,

(1.7)

we see that the residual term
\mathbf{v}_{N}=\{v_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
satisfies the following SdNLW system:

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)v_{N,j}&=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{N,k}^{2}v_{N,j}+2\Psi_{k}v_{N,k}v_{N,j}+v_{N,k}^{2}\Psi_{j}\\
&\hphantom{XXXXX}+:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}}\!:v_{N,j}+2v_{N,k}:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:+:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}\Psi_{j}}\!:\Big),\end{split}

(1.8)

where we have already applied Wick renormalization
to the powers of the stochastic convolution
\Psi_{j}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:&=\begin{cases}:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}}\!:\,,&\text{if }k=j,\\
\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j},&\text{if }k\neq j,\end{cases}\\
:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}\Psi_{j}}\!:&=\begin{cases}:\!{\Psi_{k}^{3}}\!:\,,&\text{if }k=j,\\
:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}}\!:\Psi_{j},&\text{if }k\neq j,\end{cases}\end{split}

(1.9)

where

:\!{\Psi_{k}^{\ell}}\!:
on the right-hand side denotes
the standard Wick power.
See
(
2.12
) and (
2.13
)
for the precise definition
via a regularization and limiting procedure.
See Subsection
2.4
for a further discussion.
Then, by setting

\displaystyle:\!{u_{N,k}^{2}u_{N,j}}\!:

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}v_{N,k}^{2}v_{N,j}+2\Psi_{k}v_{N,k}v_{N,j}+v_{N,k}^{2}\Psi_{j}

\displaystyle\quad\,+:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}}\!:v_{N,j}+2v_{N,k}:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:+:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}\Psi_{j}}\!:,

we obtain the renormalized version of

\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.1
):

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)u_{N,j}=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}:\!{u_{N,k}^{2}u_{N,j}}\!:+\,\sqrt{2}\xi_{j}.

(1.10)

As usual in practice

[
21
,
23
]
,
we say that

\mathbf{u}_{N}=\{u_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
is a solution to (
1.10
) if
u_{N,j}
,
j=1,\dots,N
, has the structure (
1.7
),
where
\mathbf{v}_{N}=\{v_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
is a solution to (
1.8
).

Before proceeding further, we introduce
some notations; see also Subsection
2.1
.
Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})=H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\times H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}).

For a function space
B
, we denote by
B^{\otimes N}
as the
N
-fold product of the space
B
with itself:
B^{\otimes N}=\underbrace{B\times\cdots\times B}_{N\text{ times}}
.
Given
a Banach space
B
and

N\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
the
\mathcal{A}_{N}B
-norm, denoting the
\ell^{2}
-average, by setting

\displaystyle\|a\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}B}^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\|a_{j}\|_{B}^{2}

(1.11)

for
a=\{a_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}\in B^{\otimes N}
.
We may write
\mathcal{A}_{N,j}
to emphasize
that the
\ell^{2}
-average is taken over the index
j
.
When
B=\mathbb{R}
, we simply set

\mathcal{A}_{N}=\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathbb{R}
.

We state pathwise local and global well-posedness of
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.10
).

Theorem 1.1
.

(i)

Let
m>0
and
\frac{1}{2}\leq s<1
.
Then, given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.10
) is locally well-posed
in
\big(\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}
.
More precisely,
given
\big(\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(0)},\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(1)}\big)=\big\{\big(v^{(0)}_{N,j},v^{(1)}_{N,j}\big)\big\}_{j=1}^{N}\in\big(\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}
,
there exists
a unique solution

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{N})=\{(v_{N,j},\partial_{t}v_{N,j})\}_{j=1}^{N}\in\mathcal{A}_{N}C([0,\tau];\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))

(1.12)

to (
1.8
)
with
(\mathbf{v}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{N})|_{t=0}=\big(\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(0)},\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(1)}\big)

on the time interval
[0,\tau]

for some almost surely positive time
\tau>0
.

(ii)

Let
m>0
and
\frac{4}{5}<s<1
.
Then, given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.10
) is globally well-posed
in
\big(\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}
.
More precisely,
given
\big(\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(0)},\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(1)}\big)=\big\{\big(v_{N,j}^{(0)},v_{N,j}^{(1)}\big)\big\}_{j=1}^{N}\in\big(\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}
,
there exists a unique global-in-time solution

(\mathbf{v}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{N})\in\big(C(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))\big)^{\otimes N}

to (
1.8
) with initial data

\big(\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(0)},\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(1)}\big)
,
almost surely.

Theorem
1.1

follows from a straightforward modification
of the corresponding results in the scalar case (i.e.
N=1
)
in
[
23
]
.
We present its proof in Section
3
.
The local well-posedness result
(Theorem
1.1
(i))
follows from Proposition
3.1

on local well-posedness of the perturbed SdNLW system (
3.2
)
with
Lemma
2.5

on the regularity properties
of the Wick powers of the stochastic convolution
\Psi_{j}

defined in (
1.6
).
Our proof does not make use of any auxiliary function space
and thus the uniqueness of a solution

(\mathbf{v}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{N})

holds in the entire class (
1.12
).
See Remark
3.2
(i).
We also point out that our argument yields
a uniform (in
N
)
local existence time (in the sense of Proposition
3.1
).

Let us now turn our attention
to
the pathwise global well-posedness result
(Theorem
1.1
(ii)).
Given

(\mathbf{u}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})=\{(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})\}_{j=1}^{N}
,
define the energy functional

E_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})

by setting

\displaystyle\begin{split}E_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})&=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}|\nabla u_{N,j}|^{2}+mu_{N,j}^{2}+(\partial_{t}u_{N,j})^{2}dx\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{4N^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{N,j}^{2}\Big)^{2}dx\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\|\nabla u_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}}^{2}+m\|u_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}u_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}}^{2}dx\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\|u_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}}^{4}dx,\end{split}

(1.13)

where
\mathcal{A}_{N}=\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathbb{R}
is as in (
1.11
).
The energy functional

E_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})

is obtained
by simply replacing the absolute value (= the norm on
\mathbb{R}
)
in the energy:

\displaystyle E(u,\partial_{t}u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}|\nabla u|^{2}+mu^{2}+(\partial_{t}u)^{2}dx+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}u^{4}dx

(1.14)

for the scalar (deterministic) NLW:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+m-\Delta)u=-u^{3}

(1.15)

by the
\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathbb{R}
-norm.
As such, it is easy to check that

E_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})
is conserved
under the following (deterministic) NLW system:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+m-\Delta)u_{N,j}=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}u_{N,k}^{2}u_{N,j},\qquad j=1,\dots,N.

(1.16)

With
\mathbf{v}_{N}=\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N}
, we can write (
1.16
)
as
the following Hamiltonian formulation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{u}_{N}\\
\mathbf{v}_{N}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\textbf{Id}_{N}\\
-\textbf{Id}_{N}&0\end{pmatrix}\nabla_{(\mathcal{A}_{N}L^{2}_{x})^{\otimes 2}}E_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\mathbf{v}_{N}),

(1.17)

where
\textbf{Id}_{N}
is the
N\times N
identity matrix
and

\nabla_{(\mathcal{A}_{N}L^{2}_{x})^{\otimes 2}}E_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\mathbf{v}_{N})
is the
L^{2}
-gradient
(= the Riesz representation of the Fréchet derivative of

E_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\mathbf{v}_{N})
)
with respect to the
\big(\mathcal{A}_{N}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes 2}
-inner product.

As observed in
[
23
]
,
there are two difficulties
in proving global well-posedness
of (
1.8
)
(see Proposition
3.7
),
using the energy

E_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})
in (
1.13
).
First of all,
due to the roughness of the space-time white noises
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
,
the local-in-time solution
(\mathbf{v}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{N})(t)

constructed in Theorem
1.1
(i)
does
not
belong to

\big(\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}
,
namely

E_{N}(\mathbf{v}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{N})(t)=\infty

for
t>0
, almost surely.
The second issue comes from the fact that

(\mathbf{v}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{N})

does not solve the NLW system (
1.16
)
but it satisfies
the perturbed NLW system (
1.8
),
where we view the terms involving (the Wick powers of)
\Psi_{j}

as a perturbation.
If each of these difficulties
appears alone, then we could overcome the issue
by applying the
I
-method
[
11
]
in the former case,
and by applying a Gronwall-type argument after
Burq and Tzvetkov
[
10
]
in the latter case.
In our current setting, however,
we need to handle both of the difficulties at the same time.
Our strategy is to adapt the hybrid approach
combining the
I
-method with a Gronwall-type argument,
introduced in
[
23
]

by Gubinelli, Koch, Tolomeo, and the third author,
to the current vector-valued setting.
In Subsections
3.2

and
3.3
,
we present details of a proof of Theorem
1.1
(ii)
by establishing uniform (in
N
) estimates.
Our argument also yields
a uniform (in
N
) double exponential growth bound; see Remark
3.10
.

Next, we turn our attention to the well-posedness
issue of the mean-field SdNLW (
1.2
).
Take
N\to\infty
in (
1.8
).
Heuristically, in view of the law of large numbers
(see Subsection
5.1
),
the empirical average

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq k\leq N}
in (
1.8
)
gets replaced by
an expectation
\mathbb{E}
.
Thus, in the limit as
N\to\infty
,
we formally obtain the following limiting mean-field equation:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)v_{j}=-\mathbb{E}[v_{j}^{2}]v_{j}-2\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{j}v_{j}]v_{j}-\mathbb{E}[v_{j}^{2}]\Psi_{j}-2\mathbb{E}[v_{j}\Psi_{j}]\Psi_{j},

(1.18)

We note that the fourth and sixth terms on the right-hand side
of (
1.8
) vanish in the limit,
since they (formally)
converge
to

\mathbb{E}[:\!{\Psi_{j}^{2}}\!:]v_{j}

and
\mathbb{E}[:\!{\Psi_{j}^{2}}\!:]\Psi_{j}
, respectively,
which are both
0

in view of

\mathbb{E}[:\!{\Psi_{j}^{2}}\!:]=0
.
By setting

\displaystyle u_{j}=\Psi_{j}+v_{j},

(1.19)

we obtain the following renormalized version of the mean-field SdNLW (
1.2
):

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)u_{j}=-\mathbb{E}[u_{j}^{2}-\Psi_{j}^{2}]\,u_{j}+\sqrt{2}\xi_{j}.

(1.20)

As in the
\text{HLSM}_{N}
case,
we say that

u_{j}
is a solution to (
1.20
) if
u_{j}
has the structure (
1.19
),
where
v_{j}
is a solution to (
1.18
).

Next, we state local and global well-posedness
of the mean-field SdNLW (
1.20
).

Theorem 1.2
.

(i)
Let
m>0
,
\frac{1}{2}\leq s<1
,
and
2\leq p<\infty
.
Then,
the mean-field SdNLW (
1.20
) is locally well-posed
in
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
.
More precisely,
given

(v^{(0)},v^{(1)})\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
,
there exists a unique solution

\displaystyle(v,\partial_{t}v)\in L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,\tau];\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})))

(1.21)

to (
1.18
)
with
(v,\partial_{t}v)|_{t=0}=(v^{(0)},v^{(1)})

on the time interval
[0,\tau]
for some
\tau>0
.

(ii)

Let
m>0
and
\frac{4}{5}<s<1
.
Then,
the mean-field SdNLW (
1.20
) is globally well-posed
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
.
More precisely,
given any

(v^{(0)},v^{(1)})\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))

and
T>0
,
there exists a unique global-in-time solution

(v,\partial_{t}v)\in L^{2}(\Omega;C([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})))

to (
1.18
)
with
(v,\partial_{t}v)|_{t=0}=(v^{(0)},v^{(1)})
.

We note that
the uniqueness of a solution
(v,\partial_{t}v)
to (
1.18
)
holds in the entire class (
1.21
).
See Remark
4.2
(i).
As in the case of
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.10
)
(Theorem
1.1
),
Theorem
1.2

follows from adapting the argument for the scalar case
[
23
]

to the current mean-field setting.
There is, however, one
seemingly problematic term

\mathbb{E}[v_{j}\Psi_{j}]\Psi_{j}

in (
1.18
) which does not come with renormalization.
Such a term does not appear in the scalar case
[
23
]

or in (
1.8
),
representing
a new aspect of the current mean-field model,
as compared to
the hyperbolic
O(N)
linear sigma model.
See a proof of
Proposition
4.1
.
See also Subsection
4.2

for a globalization argument
where we encounter similar issues, specific
to the current mean-field setting.

As for the global well-posedness (Theorem
1.2
(ii)),
the main strategy is to adapt
the hybrid
I
-method approach
[
23
]

to the current mean-field setting, starting
with the following energy functional:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{E}(u,\partial_{t}u)&=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}|\nabla u|^{2}+mu^{2}+(\partial_{t}u)^{2}dx\bigg]+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\big(\mathbb{E}[u^{2}]\big)^{2}dx\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}^{2}+m\|u\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}u\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}^{2}dx+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\|u\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}^{4}dx.\end{split}

(1.22)

Note that
the energy
\mathcal{E}(u,\partial_{t}u)

is obtained
by simply replacing the absolute value (= the norm on
\mathbb{R}
)
in the definition (
1.14
) of the energy
E(u,\partial_{t}u)

for the scalar (deterministic) NLW (
1.15
)
by the
L^{2}(\Omega)
-norm.
It is easy to check that

\mathcal{E}(u,\partial_{t}u)
is conserved
under the mean-field NLW:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+m-\Delta)u=-\mathbb{E}[u^{2}]u.

(1.23)

Remark 1.3
.

With
v=\partial_{t}u
, (
1.23
)
is equivalent to the following Hamiltonian formulation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u\\
v\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\nabla_{(L^{2}_{\omega,x})^{\otimes 2}}\mathcal{E}(u,v),

where

\nabla_{(L^{2}_{\omega,x})^{\otimes 2}}\mathcal{E}(u,v)
is the
L^{2}
-gradient
with respect to the
\big(L^{2}(\Omega;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))\big)^{\otimes 2}
-inner product.

Finally, we are ready to state a convergence result
for
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.10
) to the mean-field SdNLW (
1.20
)
with general initial data (under some assumptions).

Theorem 1.4
.

(i)

Let
m>0
and
\frac{4}{5}<s<1
.
Let

\big\{\big(u_{N,j}^{(0)},u_{N,j}^{(1)}\big)\big\}_{j=1}^{N}\in\big(\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
and

\big\{\big(u_{j}^{(0)},u_{j}^{(1)}\big)\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in\big(\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}

be
sequences of random functions
such that

•

\big(u_{j}^{(0)},u_{j}^{(1)}\big)\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
are independent and identically distributed,

•

for each fixed
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\big(u_{N,j}^{(0)}-u_{j}^{(0)},u_{N,j}^{(1)}-u_{j}^{(1)}\big)\big\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0

(1.24)

in probability as
N\to\infty
, and

\displaystyle\big\|\big(u_{N,j}^{(0)}-u_{j}^{(0)},u_{N,j}^{(1)}-u_{j}^{(1)}\big)\big\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0

(1.25)

in probability as
N\to\infty
.

Let

\{(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})\}_{j=1}^{N}

and
\{(u_{j},\partial_{t}u_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

be the global-in-time solutions to (
1.10
)
and (
1.20
)
with initial data
\big\{\big(u_{N,j}^{(0)},u_{N,j}^{(1)}\big)\big\}_{j=1}^{N}

and
\big\{\big(u_{j}^{(0)},u_{j}^{(1)}\big)\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

constructed in Theorems
1.1

and
1.2
, respectively.
Then, given any
T>0
, we have, for each fixed
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\|(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})-(u_{j},\partial_{t}u_{j})\|_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\longrightarrow 0

(1.26)

in probability as
N\to\infty
, and

\displaystyle\|(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})-(u_{j},\partial_{t}u_{j})\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\longrightarrow 0

(1.27)

in probability as
N\to\infty
.

(ii)

Let
m>0
and
\frac{1}{2}\leq s<1
.
Let

\big\{\big(u_{N,j}^{(0)},u_{N,j}^{(1)}\big)\big\}_{j=1}^{N}\in\big(\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
and

\big\{\big(u_{j}^{(0)},u_{j}^{(1)}\big)\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in\big(\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}

be sequences of random functions
such that

•

\big(u_{j}^{(0)},u_{j}^{(1)}\big)\in L^{6}(\Omega;\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
are independent and identically distributed,

•

there exists small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that,
for each fixed
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\big\|\big(u_{N,j}^{(0)}-u_{j}^{(0)},u_{N,j}^{(1)}-u_{j}^{(1)}\big)\big\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0

(1.28)

in probability as
N\to\infty
, and

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\big\|\big(u_{N,j}^{(0)}-u_{j}^{(0)},u_{N,j}^{(1)}-u_{j}^{(1)}\big)\big\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0

(1.29)

in probability as
N\to\infty
.

Let

\{(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})\}_{j=1}^{N}

and
\{(u_{j},\partial_{t}u_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

be the local-in-time solutions to (
1.10
)
and (
1.20
)
with initial data
\big\{\big(u_{N,j}^{(0)},u_{N,j}^{(1)}\big)\big\}_{j=1}^{N}

and
\big\{\big(u_{j}^{(0)},u_{j}^{(1)}\big)\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

constructed in Theorems
1.1

and
1.2
, respectively.
Suppose that
u_{j}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, exists on the time interval
[0,T]

for some
T>0

and that

\displaystyle v_{j}=u_{j}-\Psi_{j}\in L^{6}(\Omega;C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))),\qquad j\in\mathbb{N}.

(1.30)

Then, we have for each fixed
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})-(u_{j},\partial_{t}u_{j})\|_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\longrightarrow 0

(1.31)

in probability as
N\to\infty
, and

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})-(u_{j},\partial_{t}u_{j})\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\longrightarrow 0

(1.32)

in probability as
N\to\infty
.

Theorem
1.4
(i)
establishes global-in-time convergence
of
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.10
) to the mean-field SdNLW
(
1.20
)
but it does not provide a rate of convergence.
On the other hand,
Theorem
1.4
(ii)
only establishes local-in-time convergence
but it provides a convergence rate of
N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{0}}
;
see (
1.31
) and (
1.32
).
In view of Theorem
1.2
(i),
we note that
the higher moment assumption (
1.30
)
indeed holds true, at least for small
T>0

(and hence is not an assumption
for short times).

We present a proof of
Theorem
1.4
in Section
5
.
The main difficulty for proving
the global-in-time convergence result (Theorem
1.4
(i))
comes from the lack of a higher moment control
on a global solution
v_{j}

to the mean-field SdNLW (
1.18
).
Namely,
due to the use of the energy
\mathcal{E}(v_{j},\partial_{t}v_{j})
in (
1.22
)
which comes with the
L^{2}(\Omega)
-norm,
Theorem
1.2
(ii)
only provides the second moment bound
for the residual part
v_{j}=u_{j}-\Psi_{j}
.
This is in sharp contrast to the
parabolic setting studied in
[
47
]
,
where a higher moment bound
is available;
see
[
47
, Lemma 3.4]
.
We overcome this difficulty
in the current hyperbolic / dispersive problem
by directly establishing
law-of-large-numbers type lemmas
(Lemmas
5.1
,
5.2
, and
5.4
)
in Subsection
5.1
,
assuming only the second moment bound on
v_{j}
.
We note that, due to the lack of a higher moment control,
we can not rely on a simple orthogonality argument (as in Lemmas
5.5
and
5.6
;
see a discussion below)
and thus we need to proceed with more care,
invoking the (strong) law of large numbers
in the Banach space setting; see
[
26
, Chapter 7]
.
Once we prove
the key
law-of-large-numbers type lemmas
(Lemmas
5.1
,
5.2
, and
5.4
),
Theorem
1.4
(i)
follows from
a modification of the pathwise local well-posedness
argument
with Lemmas
5.1
,
5.2
, and
5.4
;
see Subsection
5.2
for details.
Note that, in the parabolic case
[
47
]
,
the convergence was shown by
an energy estimate
which is not available
in the current hyperbolic / dispersive setting
(recall that the energy is almost surely infinite
since
(v_{N,j}(t),\partial_{t}v_{N,j}(t))\notin\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,
almost surely).

We point out that, in handling the term
I_{3}^{N}
in
[
47
, (4.5)]
,
the authors also established a law-of-large-numbers type result;
see
[
47
, Step 4 on p. 163]
.
Their argument, however, relies on a square root gain
via orthogonality
(see
[
47
, (4.1)]
)
which requires a higher moment bound.
In proving
Theorem
1.4
(ii)
under the higher moment assumption,
we also
establish
law-of-large-numbers type lemmas
(Lemmas
5.5
and
5.6
)
but their proofs are much simpler
than those in Subsection
5.1

thanks to the higher moment control,
which allows us to
exploit orthogonality
and obtain a square root gain
just as in
[
47
]
.
See Subsection
5.3
.

Remark 1.5
.

The results stated in this subsection
(Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
,
and
1.4
)
hold for general initial data (under suitable assumptions).
In particular, we do not make use of invariant Gibbs measures.
As such,
the damping term
\partial_{t}u_{N,j}

and

\sqrt{2}
on the noises in (
1.10
)
(and also in (
1.8
), (
1.18
), and (
1.20
))
do not play any role,
and the corresponding results
hold for the undamped case.
We also note that
these results also hold for the case
m=0
(without the damping term), where a separate (but not difficult) analysis at the frequency
0
is required; see, for example,
[
21
]
.
In the next subsection,
we consider invariant Gibbs dynamics,
for which the damping term,

\sqrt{2}
on the noises,
and the positivity of
m
are all necessary.
We chose to treat the damped case
in this subsection
such that we have a uniform presentation throughout the paper.

1.3.
Large
N
limit of the invariant Gibbs dynamics

In this subsection,
we consider
the hyperbolic
O(N)
linear sigma model (
1.10
) and its mean-field limit (
1.20
)
at Gibbs equilibria.

The Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{N}
for the
coupled NLW system
(
1.16
)
is formally given by

\displaystyle d\vec{\rho}_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})=Z_{N}^{-1}e^{-N\cdot E_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})}d\mathbf{u}_{N}d(\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N}),

where

(\mathbf{u}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})=\{(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})\}_{j=1}^{N}

and

E_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})
is as in (
1.13
).
Let us rewrite
\vec{\rho}_{N}
as a weighted Gaussian measure in the following.
Given
m>0
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
define the fractional
Gaussian field
\mu_{s}
with mass
m>0
:

\displaystyle d\mu_{s}=Z^{-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\|(m-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigg)du.

(1.33)

Recall that
\mu_{s}
is a Gaussian probability measure
on
H^{s-1-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\setminus H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.
When
s=1
,
it corresponds to the massive Gaussian free field
\mu_{1}

defined in (
1.5
),
while it corresponds to the white noise measure when
s=0
.
With this notation,
we can write
\vec{\rho}_{N}
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&d\vec{\rho}_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})\\
&\quad=Z_{N}^{-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{4N}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{N,j}^{2}\Big)^{2}dx\bigg)d\mu_{1}^{\otimes N}(\mathbf{u}_{N})\otimes d\mu_{0}^{\otimes N}(\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})\\
&\quad=d\rho_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N})\otimes d\mu_{0}^{\otimes N}(\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N}),\end{split}

(1.34)

where

\rho_{N}

is the

O(N)
linear sigma model in (
1.3
).
Using the notation in (
1.17
)
(with
\mathbf{v}_{N}=\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N}
),
let us now rewrite
the (formal)
3
3

3

Namely, without renormalization.

\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.1
)
as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{u}_{N}\\
\mathbf{v}_{N}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\textbf{Id}_{N}\\
-\textbf{Id}_{N}&0\end{pmatrix}\nabla_{(\mathcal{A}_{N}L^{2}_{x})^{\otimes 2}}E_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\mathbf{v}_{N})+\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\mathbf{v}_{N}+\sqrt{2}\,\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N},\end{pmatrix}

where
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}=\{\xi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
.
Namely,

\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.1
)
is written as a superposition
of the coupled NLW system (
1.16
)
and
an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process (for the
\mathbf{v}_{N}
-component),
both of which (formally) preserves
the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{N}
.
As a result, we see that
the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{N}

in (
1.34
)
is (formally) invariant under the dynamics of

\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.1
)
(which can be made rigorous with a renormalization
and a frequency-truncation; see
[
23
, Section 4]
).

Note that
\mu_{1}^{\otimes N}

in (
1.34
) is supported on (vectors of) distributions
of negative regularity.
Namely, the interaction potential in (
1.34
)
requires a renormalization.
At the level of
the

O(N)
linear sigma model, this leads to

\displaystyle d\rho_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N})

\displaystyle=Z_{N}^{-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{4N}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}:\!{\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{N,j}^{2}\Big)^{2}}\!:dx\bigg)d\mu_{1}^{\otimes N}(\mathbf{u}_{N}),

(1.35)

where

\displaystyle:\!{\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{N,j}^{2}\Big)^{2}}\!:=\sum_{k,j=1}^{N}:\!{u_{N,k}^{2}u_{N,j}^{2}}\!:

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}:\!{u_{N,k}^{2}u_{N,j}^{2}}\!:&=\begin{cases}:\!{u_{N,k}^{4}}\!:\,,&\text{if }k=j,\\
:\!{u_{N,k}^{2}}\!:\,:\!{u_{N,j}^{2}}\!:,&\text{if }k\neq j.\end{cases}\end{split}

Here,

:\!{u_{k}^{\ell}}\!:
on the right-hand side denotes
the standard Wick power.
By adapting the argument from the scalar case
(
[
29
,
13
,
40
]
) to the current vector-valued setting,
we can realize
\rho_{N}
in (
1.35
)
as a probability measure
on
\big(H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}
;
see also
[
47
, Remark 5.8]
.
The renormalized Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{N}=\rho_{N}\otimes\mu_{0}^{\otimes N}

(wth
\rho_{N}
in (
1.35
)) is then given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&d\vec{\rho}_{N}(\mathbf{u}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N})\\
&\quad=Z_{N}^{-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{4N}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}:\!{\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{N,j}^{2}\Big)^{2}}\!:dx\bigg)d\mu_{1}^{\otimes N}(\mathbf{u}_{N})\otimes d\mu_{0}^{\otimes N}(\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{N}).\end{split}

(1.36)

Let us now turn our attention to the dynamical problem.
Let

\big\{\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

be a family of independent
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
-valued random variables,
independent of the family
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

of the space-time white noises,
satisfying

\displaystyle\Law\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j}\big)=\mu_{1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Law\big(\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)=\mu_{0},\quad j\in\mathbb{N},

(1.37)

where
\mu_{s}
is as in
(
1.33
).
Here,
\Law(X)
denotes the law of a random variable
X
.
Given
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
let

\Phi_{j}
be the solution to the following linear stochastic damped wave equation:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)\Phi_{j}=\sqrt{2}\xi_{j}\\
(\Phi_{j},\partial_{t}\Phi_{j})|_{t=0}=\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big).\end{cases}

(1.38)

A straightforward computation shows that

\displaystyle\Law(\Phi_{j}(t),\partial_{t}\Phi_{j}(t))=\Law\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)=\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{0}

for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Our goal is to study the convergence issue
of (the renormalized version of)
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.1
) with the Gibbsian initial data:

\displaystyle\Law\Big(\{(u_{N,j}(0),\partial_{t}u_{N,j}(0))\}_{j=1}^{N}\Big)=\vec{\rho}_{N}=\rho_{N}\otimes\mu_{0}^{\otimes N},

(1.39)

which
we assume to be independent of the family
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

of the space-time white noises.
Moreover, we impose that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{N,j}(0)=\phi_{j}^{(1)},\quad j=1,\dots,N.

(1.40)

By the first order expansion:

\displaystyle u_{N,j}=\Phi_{j}+v_{N,j},

the residual term
v_{N,j}
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)v_{N,j}&=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{N,k}^{2}v_{N,j}+2\Phi_{k}v_{N,k}v_{N,j}+v_{N,k}^{2}\Phi_{j}\\
&\hphantom{XXXXX}+:\!{\Phi_{k}^{2}}\!:v_{N,j}+2v_{N,k}:\!{\Phi_{k}\Phi_{j}}\!:+:\!{\Phi_{k}^{2}\Phi_{j}}\!:\Big)\\
\end{split}

(1.41)

with initial data (recall (
1.40
))

\displaystyle(v_{N,j},\partial_{t}v_{N,j})|_{t=0}=\big(v_{N,j}^{(0)},0\big)=\big(u_{N,j}(0)-\phi_{j}^{(0)},0\big),

where we have already applied Wick renormalization
to the powers of the stochastic convolution
\Phi_{j}
;
see (
2.15
) for the more precise definition.
Then, by setting

\displaystyle:\!{u_{N,k}^{2}u_{N,j}}\!:

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}v_{N,k}^{2}v_{N,j}+2\Phi_{k}v_{N,k}v_{N,j}+v_{N,k}^{2}\Phi_{j}

\displaystyle\quad\,+:\!{\Phi_{k}^{2}}\!:v_{N,j}+2v_{N,k}:\!{\Phi_{k}\Phi_{j}}\!:+:\!{\Phi_{k}^{2}\Phi_{j}}\!:,

we obtain the renormalized version of

\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.1
):

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)u_{N,j}=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}:\!{u_{N,k}^{2}u_{N,j}}\!:+\sqrt{2}\xi_{j}

(1.42)

with the Gibbsian initial data (
1.39
).
In the current setting, the limiting mean-field equation is given by

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)u_{j}=-\mathbb{E}[u_{j}^{2}-\Phi_{j}^{2}]\,u_{j}+\sqrt{2}\xi_{j}.

(1.43)

Compare (
1.43
) with (
1.20
).
Consider (
1.43
)
with initial data
\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)
.
We first note that
u_{j}\equiv\Phi_{j}

satisfies (
1.43
)
with
(u_{j},\partial_{t}u_{j})=\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)
.
Under the first order expansion

u_{j}=\Phi_{j}+v_{j}
,
this implies that

(v_{j},\partial_{t}v_{j})\equiv(0,0)

is a global-in-time solution to

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)v_{j}=-\mathbb{E}[v_{j}^{2}]v_{j}-2\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{j}v_{j}]v_{j}-\mathbb{E}[v_{j}^{2}]\Phi_{j}-2\mathbb{E}[v_{j}\Phi_{j}]\Phi_{j}

(1.44)

with zero initial data,
which trivially implies

\displaystyle(v_{j},\partial_{t}v_{j})\in L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})))

(1.45)

for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
T>0
.
By noting that Theorem
1.2

also applies to (
1.43
) and (
1.44
)
and iteratively applying
Theorem
1.2
(i),
we then conclude that

(u_{j},\partial_{t}u_{j})\equiv(\Phi_{j},\partial_{t}\Phi_{j})
is the unique solution
in the class:

\displaystyle(\Phi_{j},\partial_{t}\Phi_{j})+L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})))

(1.46)

for any

\frac{1}{2}\leq s<1

and
T>0
.

We now state a convergence result
of the invariant Gibbs dynamics
of
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.42
).

Theorem 1.6
.

(i)

Let
m>0
and
\frac{4}{5}<s<1
.
Then, given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.42
) is globally well-posed
in
\big(\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}
.
More precisely,
given
\big(\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(0)},\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(1)}\big)=\big\{\big(v_{N,j}^{(0)},v_{N,j}^{(1)}\big)\big\}_{j=1}^{N}\in\big(\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}
,
there exists a unique global-in-time solution

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{N})=\{(v_{N,j},\partial_{t}v_{N,j})\}_{j=1}^{N}\in\big(C(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))\big)^{\otimes N}

to (
1.41
) with initial data

\big(\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(0)},\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(1)}\big)
,
almost surely.

(ii)

Let
m>0
. Then, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{N}=\rho_{N}\otimes\mu_{0}^{\otimes N}

defined in (
1.36
) is invariant under the dynamics of (
1.42
). More precisely, for each
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have

\Law\Big(\{(u_{N,j}(t),\partial_{t}u_{N,j}(t))\}_{j=1}^{N}\Big)=\vec{\rho}_{N}.

(iii)

Let
m>0
.
There exists a probability space
(\Omega^{\prime},\mathbb{P}^{\prime})

such that
the following statements hold.

(iii.a)

There exists a family

\big\{\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

of
independent
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
-valued random variables
on
\Omega^{\prime}
,
independent of the family
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

of the space-time white noises,
satisfying (
1.37
).

(iii.b)

For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
there exists a family
\{v^{(0)}_{N,j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

of
H^{1}
-valued
random variables
on
\Omega^{\prime}
,
independent of the family
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

of the space-time white noises, satisfying

\displaystyle\sup_{j=1,\dots,N}\Big\|\big\|v^{(0)}_{N,j}\big\|_{H^{1}_{x}}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\prime})}\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(1.47)

such that

\Law\Big(\big\{\big(v^{(0)}_{N,j}+\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)\big\}_{j=1}^{N}\Big)=\vec{\rho}_{N}.

(iii.c)

Let

\{(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})\}_{j=1}^{N}

be the global-in-time solution to (
1.42
)
with the Gibbsian initial data

\big\{\big(v^{(0)}_{N,j}+\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)\big\}_{j=1}^{N}

constructed in
(iii.b)
.
Then, given any
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, we have, for each fixed
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})-(\Phi_{j},\partial_{t}\Phi_{j})\|_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\longrightarrow 0

(1.48)

in probability as
N\to\infty
, and

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})-(\Phi_{j},\partial_{t}\Phi_{j})\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\longrightarrow 0

(1.49)

in probability as
N\to\infty
.

Theorem
1.6
(iii)
implies propagation of chaos
for
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.42
).
In view of (
1.47
)
and the independence of
\big\{\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
,
the Gibbsian initial data

\big\{\big(v^{(0)}_{N,j}+\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)\big\}_{j=1}^{N}

(which is coupled)
is asymptotically chaotic
(namely, components becomes independent as
N\to\infty
).
The convergence claims (
1.48
) and (
1.49
)
show that the solution

\{(u_{N,j}(t),\partial_{t}u_{N,j}(t))\}_{j=1}^{N}

is also
asymptotically chaotic
for each
t>0
.
See

[
51
, Definition 2.1 on p. 177 and Theorem 3.1 on p. 203]

for a further discussion on propagation of chaos.

Noting that the regularity properties of
\Phi_{j}

is essentially the same as
\Psi_{j}

(see Lemmas
2.5
and
2.7
),
Theorem
1.6
(i) follows from a slight modification
of the proof of
Theorem
1.1
presented in Section
3

and thus we omit details.
As for
Theorem
1.6
(ii),
we first note that Bourgain’s invariant measure
argument
[
4
,
5
]
is not needed here
in view of the pathwise global well-posedness
(Theorem
1.6
(i)).
The claimed invariance
of the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{N}

under (
1.42
)
follows
from
the invariance of the frequency-truncated version
of the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{N}

and a PDE approximation argument;
see, for example,
[
23
,
37
]
.
As this is standard in the field by now, we omit details.

The construction of the initial data sets claimed in
Theorem
1.6
(iii.a) and (iii.b)
is based on the results in

[
47
,
14
]
(see Lemma
6.2
)
and follows from a key proposition (Proposition
6.1
).
We present details in Subsection
6.1
.
In view of (
1.45
) and (
1.47
), the convergence result
(Theorem
1.6
(iii.c))
follows from a
straightforward modification
of the proof of Theorem
1.4
(ii).
See Subsection
6.2
for details.

Remark 1.7
.

In
[
57
]
,
Tolomeo proved ergodicity of the invariant Gibbs measures
for the defocusing SdNLW on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
A slight variation of the argument in
[
57
]

yields ergodicity of the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{N}

for
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.42
).
It would be of interest to study ergodic properties of the invariant Gaussian measure

\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{0}
for the mean-field SdNLW (
1.43
)
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
Note that such a result is known in the parabolic case;
see
[
47
]
.

Remark 1.8
.

With a slight modification of the proof, Theorem
1.6

extends to
the NLW system (
1.16
) (with a proper renormalization)
with the Gibbsian initial data in (
1.39
),
converging
to its mean-field limit (
1.23
),
which is nothing but the linear dynamics:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(\partial_{t}^{2}+m-\Delta)Z_{j}=0\\
(Z_{j},\partial_{t}Z_{j})|_{t=0}=\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big).\end{cases}

with

\big\{\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

as in (
1.37
).

Remark 1.9
.

(i) In this paper, we considered the defocusing case, namely,
with the
-
sign in front of the nonlinearity

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}u_{N,k}^{2}u_{N,j}

in (
1.1
).
We note that the local-in-time results
(Theorems
1.1
(i),

1.2
(i), and

1.4
(ii))
hold true even in the focusing case
(i.e. with the
+
sign in front of the nonlinearity

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}u_{N,k}^{2}u_{N,j}

in (
1.1
)).

As for Theorem
1.6
(iii)
on the convergence of invariant Gibbs dynamics,
it does not extend to the focusing case,
since, even in the scalar case (
N=1
),
the focusing
\Phi^{4}_{2}
-measure
does not exist as a probability measure
(even with a (Wick renormalized)
L^{2}
-cutoff
(as in
[
25
,
5
]
)
or a taming by a power of the Wick renormalized
L^{2}
-norm);
see
[
8
,
38
,
20
]
.
See also

[
39
,
33
,
34
]

for an overview of the focusing Gibbs measure
(non-)construction and the references therein.
On the other hand,
in the case of the quadratic nonlinearity,
the Gibbs measure exists in the scalar case (
N=1
; see
[
38
]
),
which seems to extend to the
O(N)
linear sigma model
(with a taming by a power of the Wick renormalized
L^{2}
-norm,
which is suitable for studying wave dynamics).
Thus, it would be of interest to investigate if an analogue
of Theorem
1.6
(iii) holds in this case.

(ii) In the survey paper
[
46
, Section 9]
,
Shen
proposed to study the case of infinite volume,
where there is no known result at this point.
In the wave case, thanks to the finite speed of propagation,
we may combine the ideas
from
[
56
]
and the current paper
to extend
Theorem
1.4

for general initial data
to
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.10
) posed on the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We will address this issue
in a forthcoming work.

2.
Notations and preliminary lemmas

In Subsections
2.1

and
2.2
,
we introduce basic notations
and recall the product estimates.
In Subsection
2.3
,
we then
recall the definition of the
I
-operator
along with its basic properties
and commutator estimates.
In Subsection
2.4
,
we go over the
definitions of the stochastic convolutions

\Psi_{j}

and
\Phi_{j}

in (
1.6
)
and (
1.38
), respectively,
and their Wick powers
along with their regularity properties.

2.1.
Notations

Throughout this paper, the value of mass
m>0

is fixed and thus we often suppress
m
-dependence
in the remaining part of the paper.
In describing regularities of functions and distributions,
we use
\varepsilon>0
to denote an arbitrarily small constant.
Constants in various estimates depend on this
\varepsilon
but,
for simplicity of notation, we suppress such
\varepsilon
-dependence.
When it is clear from the context, we often suppress
dependence on other parameters such as
s
and
t
.

For
A,B>0
, we use
A\lesssim B
to mean that
there exists
C>0
such that
A\leq CB
.
By
A\sim B
, we mean that
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
.
We may write
\lesssim_{\alpha}
and
\sim_{\alpha}
to
emphasize the dependence on an external parameter
\alpha
.
We use
A\ll B
to mean

A\leq c_{0}B

for some small
c_{0}>0
.
We use
C>0
to denote various constants, which may vary line by line,
and we may write
C_{\alpha}
or
C(\alpha)
to signify dependence on an external parameter

\alpha
.

In dealing with a solution
u
to a wave equation,
we use

\vec{u}
to denote a pair
(u,\partial_{t}u)
.
With a slight abuse of notation,
given a vector
(f_{1},\dots,f_{N})
,
we often write it as
\{f_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}

or
\vec{f}
.
We apply a similar convention for a vector

(f_{1},f_{2},\dots)
of infinite length and write it as

\{f_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}

or
\vec{f}
.
Namely, we use the vectorial notation
for two different meanings:
\vec{u}=(u,\partial_{t}u)

and
\vec{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{N})=\{f_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
,
but its meaning is clear from the context.

Given
a Banach space
B
and

N\in\mathbb{N}
,
let the
\mathcal{A}_{N}B
-norm
of
a=\{a_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}\in B^{\otimes N}

be the
\ell^{2}
-average of the
B
-norms of
a_{j}
, defined in
(
1.11
).
When there is no confusion,
we also write

\|a_{j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}B}
for
\|a\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}B}=\|a_{j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}B}
.
By Jensen’s inequality (or Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality),
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\|a_{j}\|_{B}\leq\|a\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}B}=\|a_{j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}B}.

(2.1)

Similarly,
we define the
\mathcal{A}_{N}^{(2)}B
-norm by
setting

\displaystyle\|a\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}^{(2)}B}^{2}=\|a\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}\mathcal{A}_{N,k}B}^{2}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{j,k=1}^{N}\|a_{j,k}\|_{B}^{2}

(2.2)

for
a=\{a_{j,k}\}_{j,k=1}^{N}\in B^{\otimes N^{2}}
.
When
B=\mathbb{R}
, we simply set

\mathcal{A}_{N}=\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{A}_{N}^{(2)}=\mathcal{A}_{N}^{(2)}\mathbb{R}
.

By convention, we endow

\mathbb{T}^{2}
with the normalized Lebesgue measure
dx_{{}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}}=(2\pi)^{-2}dx

such that we do not need to carry factors involving
2\pi
.
For simplicity of notation,
we use
dx
to denote
the normalized Lebesgue measure
dx_{{}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}

in the following.
We write
\widehat{f}
or
\mathcal{F}(f)=\mathcal{F}_{x}(f)
to denote the Fourier transform of
f
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
Given
M\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\mathbf{P}_{M}
the
(spatial) frequency projector onto frequencies
\{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:|n|\leq M\}
and
we set

\mathbf{P}_{M}^{\perp}=\textbf{Id}-\mathbf{P}_{M}
.

We use

\mathcal{D}(t)

to denote
the linear damped wave propagator
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(t)=e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\frac{\sin(t[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt])}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt]},

(2.3)

where

\displaystyle[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt]=[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt]_{m}=\sqrt{m-\frac{1}{4}-\Delta}.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, we also set

\displaystyle[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt]=[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt]_{m}=\sqrt{m-\frac{1}{4}+|n|^{2}}.

(When
0<m\leq\frac{1}{4}
,
the operator
\mathcal{D}(t)
in (
2.3
) behaves slightly differently at the frequency
n=0
.
However, it does not affect our analysis.)
Given
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
consider the following linear damped wave equation
(with a nice function
F
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
):

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)u=F\\
(u,\partial_{t}u)|_{t=t_{0}}=(f,g).\end{cases}

(2.4)

Then, a solution
u
to (
2.4
)
is
given by

\displaystyle u(t)=S(t-t_{0})(f,g)+\mathcal{I}_{t_{0}}(F)(t),

where
S(t)
is the linear propagator given by

\displaystyle S(t)(f,g)=\partial_{t}\mathcal{D}(t)f+\mathcal{D}(t)(f+g)

(2.5)

and
\mathcal{I}_{t_{0}}
denotes the damped wave Duhamel integral operator given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{t_{0}}(f)(t)=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})f(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}e^{-\frac{t-t^{\prime}}{2}}\frac{\sin((t-t^{\prime})[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt])}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt]}f(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}.

(2.6)

When
t_{0}=0
,
we set
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{0}

for simplicity.

Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq r\leq\infty
, we define the
L^{r}
-based Sobolev space
W^{s,r}(\mathbb{T}^{2})

as the closure of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
under
the norm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{W^{s,r}}=\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}f\|_{L^{r}}=\big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big(\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{s}\widehat{f}\big)\big\|_{L^{r}}

such that
W^{s,r}(\mathbb{T}^{2})

is separable even when
r=\infty
.
Here,

\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle=(1+|\cdot|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
When
r=2
, we set
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})=W^{s,2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.
We also define
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})=H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\times H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
via the norm:

\displaystyle\|(f,g)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}=\|f\|_{H^{s}}+\|g\|_{H^{s-1}}.

We will often use the short-hand notations
such as
C_{T}H^{s}_{x}
and
L^{p}_{\omega}H^{s}_{x}
for
C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
and
L^{p}(\Omega;H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
, respectively.
Given an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
,
we also write

C_{I}H_{x}^{s}
for
C(I;H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
, etc.
Lastly, given
1\leq p\leq\infty
, we denote by
p^{\prime}
its Hölder conjugate (i.e.
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
).

2.2.
Product estimates

We recall the following product estimates for Sobolev spaces;
see
[
3
]
for a proof. See also
[
21
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
s>0
.

(i) (fractional Leibniz rule).
Let
1<p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2},r\leq\infty
be such that
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{1}}=\frac{1}{p_{2}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{W^{s,r}}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{s,p_{1}}}\|g\|_{L^{q_{1}}}+\|f\|_{L^{p_{2}}}\|g\|_{W^{s,q_{2}}}.

(ii)
Let
1<p\leq\infty
and
1<q,r<\infty
be such that

\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}\leq\frac{s}{2}+\frac{1}{r}
and
q,r^{\prime}\geq p^{\prime}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{W^{-s,r}}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{-s,p}}\|g\|_{W^{s,q}}.

2.3.
I
-operator and basic commutator estimates

Fix
0<s<1
.
Given
M\in\mathbb{N}
, we define a
smooth, radially symmetric, non-increasing (in
|\xi|
)
multiplier
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{s,M}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2};[0,1])
, satisfying

\mathfrak{m}_{s,M}(\xi)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }|\xi|\leq M,\\
\big(\frac{M}{|\xi|}\big)^{1-s},&\text{if }|\xi|\geq 2M.\end{cases}

We then define the
I
-operator
I=I_{s,M}

to be the Fourier multiplier operator with the multiplier
\mathfrak{m}_{s,M}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{I_{s,M}f}(n)=\mathfrak{m}_{s,M}(n)\widehat{f}(n),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

(2.7)

As it is customary in the field,
we suppress the
s
-dependence
and
usually use
I
(or
I_{M}
to denote
the
M
-dependence in an explicit manner) in the following,
Note that

If\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
if and only if
f\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})

with the bound:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\|If\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim M^{1-s}\|f\|_{H^{s}}.

(2.8)

Moreover,
from the Littlewood-Paley theorem, we have

\displaystyle\|If\|_{W^{s_{0}+s_{1},p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim M^{s_{1}}\|f\|_{W^{s_{0},p}}

(2.9)

for any
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
0\leq s_{1}\leq 1-s
, and
1<p<\infty
.

We now recall the commutator estimates.
See
[
23
, Lemmas 3.1 and 3.3]

for the proofs.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\frac{2}{3}\leq s<1
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\|I(f^{2}g)-(If)^{2}Ig\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim M^{2-3s}\|If\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\|Ig\|_{H^{1}},

where the implicit constant is independent of
M\in\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\frac{2}{3}\leq s<1
.
Then, given small
\delta>0
, there exist small
\sigma_{0}=\sigma_{0}(\delta)>0
and
p_{0}=p_{0}(\delta)\gg 1
such that

\displaystyle\|I(fh)-(If)(Ih)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim M^{-\frac{s}{2}+\delta}\|If\|_{H^{1}}\|h\|_{W^{-\sigma_{0},p_{0}}},

\displaystyle\|I(fgh)-(If)(Ig)(Ih)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim M^{-s+\frac{1}{2}+\delta}\|If\|_{H^{1}}\|Ig\|_{H^{1}}\|h\|_{W^{-\sigma_{0},p_{0}}}

for any sufficiently large
M\gg 1
,
where the implicit constant is independent of

M\gg 1
.

2.4.
Stochastic convolutions
and their Wick powers

In this subsection,
by first recalling some basic tools from stochastic analysis
(see, for example,
[
50
,
24
,
30
]
),
we
go over the definitions
and basic regularity properties of
the stochastic convolutions
\Psi_{j}
and
\Phi_{j}

defined in (
1.6
) and (
1.38
), respectively,
and their Wick powers,
formally defined in (
1.9
).

First,
recall the Hermite polynomials
H_{k}(x;\sigma)

of degree
k

with variance parameter
\sigma>0
,
defined through the generating function:

e^{tx-\frac{1}{2}\sigma t^{2}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}H_{k}(x;\sigma).

For readers’ convenience, we write out the first few Hermite polynomials:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&H_{0}(x;\sigma)=1,\quad H_{1}(x;\sigma)=x,\quad H_{2}(x;\sigma)=x^{2}-\sigma,\quad H_{3}(x;\sigma)=x^{3}-3\sigma x.\end{split}

Next, we recall the Wiener chaos estimate.
Let
(H,B,\mu)
be an abstract Wiener space.
Namely,
\mu
is a Gaussian measure on a separable Banach space
B

with
H\subset B
as its Cameron-Martin space.
Given a complete orthonormal system
\{e_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset B^{*}
of
H^{*}=H
,
we define a polynomial chaos of order

k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
to be an element of the form
\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}H_{k_{j}}(\langle x,e_{j}\rangle)
,
where
x\in B
,
k_{j}\neq 0
for only finitely many
j
’s,
k=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}k_{j}
,

H_{k_{j}}
is the Hermite polynomial of degree
k_{j}
,
and
\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle=\vphantom{|}_{B}\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{B^{*}}
denotes the
B
-
B^{*}
duality pairing.
We then
denote the closure of the span of
polynomial chaoses of order
k

under
L^{2}(B,\mu)
by
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.
The elements in
\mathcal{H}_{k}

are called homogeneous Wiener chaoses of order
k
.
We also set

\mathcal{H}_{\leq k}=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{k}\mathcal{H}_{j}

for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

As a consequence
of the hypercontractivity of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck
semigroup due to Nelson
[
29
]
,
we have the following Wiener chaos estimate
(
[
50
, Theorem I.22]
).
See also
[
55
, Proposition 2.4]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, we have

\|X\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq(p-1)^{\frac{k}{2}}\|X\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

for any
p\geq 2

and
X\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq k}
.

Next, we provide a precise meaning of the stochastic convolutions

\Psi_{j}
and
\Phi_{j}

and define their Wick powers.
Let
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

be a family of
independent space-time white noises on

\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}

as in (
1.1
).
Given
j\in\mathbb{N}

and
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
define
B_{n}^{j}

by setting

B_{n}^{j}(t)=\langle\xi_{j},\mathbf{1}_{[0,t]}\cdot e^{in\cdot x}\rangle_{t,x}
,
where

\langle\,\cdot,\cdot\,\rangle_{t,x}
denotes
the duality pairing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
It is easy
to see that

\{B_{n}^{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N},n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
is a family of independent complex-valued
Brownian motions conditioned that
B^{j}_{-n}=\overline{B^{j}_{n}}

for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Note that we have

\text{Var}(B^{j}_{n}(t))=\mathbb{E}\Big[\langle\xi_{j},\mathbf{1}_{[0,t]}\cdot e^{in\cdot x}\rangle_{t,x}\overline{\langle\xi_{j},\mathbf{1}_{[0,t]}\cdot e^{in\cdot x}\rangle_{t,x}}\Big]=\|\mathbf{1}_{[0,t]}\cdot e^{in\cdot x}\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}^{2}=t

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
We then define
a family

\{W^{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

of independent cylindrical Wiener processes on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})

by setting

\displaystyle W^{j}(t)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}B_{n}^{j}(t)e^{in\cdot x}.

With these notations, we can write

\Psi_{j}
in (
1.6
)
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{j}(t)&=\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})\xi_{j}(dt^{\prime})\\
&=\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})dW^{j}(t^{\prime}),\end{split}

(2.10)

where
\mathcal{D}(t)
is
the linear damped wave propagator defined in (
2.3
).
Given
j,M\in\mathbb{N}
, set
\Psi_{j,M}=\mathbf{P}_{M}\Psi_{j}
.
Then, given
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
,
we see that

\Psi_{j,M}(t,x)
is a mean-zero Gaussian random variable with variance:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sigma_{M}(t)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\big[\Psi_{j,M}^{2}(t,x)\big]\\
&\hskip 1.42262pt=\sum_{|n|\leq M}\bigg(\frac{1-e^{-t}}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt]^{2}}-\frac{e^{-t}2\sin(2t[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt])}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt](1+4[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt]^{2})}-\frac{1-e^{-t}\cos(2t[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt])}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt]^{2}(1+4[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt]^{2})}\bigg)\\
&\sim_{m,t}\log M\longrightarrow\infty,\end{split}

(2.11)

as
M\to\infty
.
Note that,
while
\sigma_{M}
depends on
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
it is independent of
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
.

Given
k,j\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
the Wick powers by setting

\displaystyle\begin{split}:\!{\Psi_{k,M}\Psi_{j,M}}\!:\,&=\begin{cases}H_{2}(\Psi_{j,M};\sigma_{M}),&\text{if }k=j,\\
\Psi_{k,M}\Psi_{j,M},&\text{if }k\neq j,\end{cases}\\
:\!{\Psi_{k,M}^{2}\Psi_{j,M}}\!:\,&=\begin{cases}H_{3}(\Psi_{j,M};\sigma_{M}),&\text{if }k=j,\\
H_{2}(\Psi_{k,M};\sigma_{M})\Psi_{j,M},&\text{if }k\neq j,\end{cases}\end{split}

(2.12)

where
H_{\ell}(x;\sigma)
is the Hermite polynomial of degree
\ell
with variance parameter
\sigma
. Then, the Wick powers, formally given in (
1.9
),
are defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:\,&=\lim_{M\to\infty}:\!{\Psi_{k,M}\Psi_{j,M}}\!:\,,\\
:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}\Psi_{j}}\!:\,&=\lim_{M\to\infty}:\!{\Psi_{k,M}^{2}\Psi_{j,M}}\!:.\end{split}

(2.13)

Proceeding as in
[
21
,
22
]
,
it is easy to see that, given any
\varepsilon>0
, the limits in (
2.13
)
exist
in
C(\mathbb{R}_{+};W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))

almost surely.
See Lemma
2.5
.

Next, we turn our attention to
\Phi_{j}
in (
1.38
).
With the notations above, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{j}(t)=S(t)\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)+\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})dW^{j}(t^{\prime}),

(2.14)

where

S(t)
is as in (
2.5
) and
\big\{\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

is a family of independent
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
-valued random variables, satisfying (
1.37
),
which is assumed to be independent of
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Given
M\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we set
\Phi_{j,M}=\mathbf{P}_{M}\Phi_{j}
.
Then,
using (
2.11
),
it is easy to see that,
given
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
,

\Phi_{j,M}(t,x)
is a mean-zero Gaussian random variable with variance:

\displaystyle\alpha_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\big[\Phi^{2}_{j,M}(t,x)\big]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\\
|n|\leq M\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{m+|n|^{2}}\sim_{m}\log M\longrightarrow\infty,

as
M\to\infty
.
Note that
\alpha_{M}
is independent of

(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
Then, as in (
2.13
), we define the Wick powers
by setting

\displaystyle\begin{split}:\!{\Phi_{k}\Phi_{j}}\!:\,&=\lim_{M\to\infty}:\!{\Phi_{k,M}\Phi_{j,M}}\!:,\\
:\!{\Phi_{k}^{2}\Phi_{j}}\!:\,&=\lim_{M\to\infty}:\!{\Phi_{k,M}^{2}\Phi_{j,M}}\!:,\end{split}

(2.15)

where the limits exist almost surely in

C(\mathbb{R}_{+};W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))

for any
\varepsilon>0
.
Here,

:\!{\Phi_{k,M}\Phi_{j,M}}\!:
and
:\!{(\Phi_{k,M})^{2}\Phi_{j,M}}\!:

are as in (
2.12
),
but with the variance parameter
\alpha_{M}
instead of
\sigma_{M}
.

Before we state lemmas on regularity properties
of the stochastic convolutions and their Wick powers, we need to introduce
some notations.
Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Given

\varepsilon>0

and an interval
J\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
we
define the space
\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(J)

of enhanced data sets

\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}
of the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}=\big\{Z_{j}^{(1)},Z_{k,j}^{(2)},Z_{k,j}^{(3)}\big\}_{j,k=1}^{N}

(2.16)

by setting

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}\|_{\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(J)}&=\|Z_{j}^{(1)}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}C_{J}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}+\|Z_{k,k}^{(2)}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}C_{J}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}\\
&\quad+\|Z_{k,j}^{(2)}\|_{\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{N}C_{J}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}+\|Z_{k,j}^{(3)}\|_{\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{N}C_{J}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}},\end{split}

(2.17)

where

\mathcal{A}_{N}B
and

\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{N}B
are
the
\ell^{2}
-averages defined
in (
1.11
) and (
2.2
).
When
J=[0,T]
,
with a slight abuse of notation, we simply set

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(T)=\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}([0,T]).

(2.18)

For our application, we take

\displaystyle Z_{j}^{(1)}=\Psi_{j},\qquad Z_{k,j}^{(2)}=\,:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z_{k,j}^{(3)}=\,:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}\Psi_{j}}\!:

(2.19)

in studying
(
1.8
),
while we set

\displaystyle Z_{j}^{(1)}=\Phi_{j},\qquad Z_{k,j}^{(2)}=\,:\!{\Phi_{k}\Phi_{j}}\!:\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z_{k,j}^{(3)}=\,:\!{\Phi_{k}^{2}\Phi_{j}}\!:

(2.20)

in studying
(
1.41
).

The first lemma states the basic regularity properties
of these stochastic terms.

Lemma 2.5
.

(i)

Given
j,k,M\in\mathbb{N}
, set

\displaystyle Z_{M}^{(1)}=\Psi_{j,M},\qquad Z_{M}^{(2)}=\,:\!{\Psi_{k,M}\Psi_{j,M}}\!:\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z_{M}^{(3)}=\,:\!{\Psi_{k,M}^{2}\Psi_{j,M}}\!:,

where

\Psi_{j,M}=\mathbf{P}_{M}\Psi_{j}
,
and

:\!{\Psi_{k,M}\Psi_{j,M}}\!:

and
:\!{\Psi_{k,M}^{2}\Psi_{j,M}}\!:

are as in
(
2.12
).
Let
\ell=1,2,3
.
Then,
given any
T,\varepsilon>0
and finite
p\geq 1
,

Z^{(\ell)}_{M}

converges to some limit
Z^{(\ell)}
in

L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})))

and also almost surely in
C(\mathbb{R}_{+};W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
,
endowed with the compact-open topology in time,
as
M\to\infty
,
where
Z^{(\ell)}
is given by

\displaystyle Z^{(1)}=\Psi_{j},\qquad Z^{(2)}=\,:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z^{(3)}=\,:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}\Psi_{j}}\!:.

Moreover, there exist

C,c>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\|Z^{(\ell)}\|_{C_{J}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}>\lambda\Big)\leq C\exp\big(-c\lambda^{\frac{2}{\ell}}\big)

(2.21)

for any
\lambda>0
and any interval
J\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
|J|=1
,
uniformly in
J
and
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ii)

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}
be as in (
2.16
),
where

Z_{j}^{(1)}
,
Z_{k,j}^{(2)}
, and
Z_{k,j}^{(3)}

are as in (
2.19
).
Then, given
\varepsilon>0
, there exist

C,c>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}\|_{\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}_{N}(J)}>\lambda\Big)\leq C\exp\Big(-c\frac{\lambda^{\frac{2}{3}}}{(1+|J|)^{\frac{2}{3}}}\Big)

(2.22)

for any
interval
J\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}

and

\lambda>0
,
uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
where the
\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(J)
-norm is as in (
2.17
).

(iii)

The claims in Parts (i) and (ii) hold
even if we replace
\Psi_{j}

and its Wick powers
by
\Phi_{j}

defined in (
2.14
)
with the corresponding Wick powers.

Proof.

(i)
By noting that
Z^{(\ell)}_{M},\,Z^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
,
the claims in Part (i)
follows from
a slight modification of
the proof of

[
23
, Lemma 2.3]
,
based on
the Wiener chaos estimate (Lemma
2.4
)
and
the Garsia-Rodemich-Rumsey inequality
(
[
23
, Lemma 2.2]
).
Since we work with the damped wave equation,
there is no growth in time
in computing the
p
th moments of
the stochastic terms,
which is the reason that the right-hand side of (
2.21
)
is independent of time;
compare this with
[
23
, (2.7)]
.
See also
[
21
, Proposition 2.1]

and

[
22
, Lemma 3.1]
,
where the latter utilizes

[
35
, Proposition 2.7 and Lemma A.1]
.

(ii)
For simplicity of the presentation,
we only consider the case
J=[0,T]
.
We first consider the contribution from the last term
on the right-hand side of (
2.17
).
Write
[0,T]=\bigcup_{\ell=0}^{[T]}J_{\ell}
,
where
J_{\ell}=[\ell,\ell+1]
for
\ell=0,\dots,[T]-1

and
J_{[T]}=\big[[T],T\big]
.
Here,
[T]
denotes the integer part of
T
.

From
(
2.21
),
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\ell=0,\dots,[T]}\sup_{j,k\in\mathbb{N}}\|Z_{k,j}^{(3)}\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}C_{J_{\ell}}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}\lesssim p^{\frac{3}{2}}.

Then, by Minkowski’s integral inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Big\|\|Z_{k,j}^{(3)}\|_{\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{N}C_{T}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}&\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{[T]}\Big\|\|Z_{k,j}^{(3)}\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}C_{J_{\ell}}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}\Big\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}^{(2)}}\\
&\lesssim p^{\frac{3}{2}}(1+T)\end{split}

(2.23)

for any finite
p\geq 2
,
where the implicit constant is independent of
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
By a crude estimate,
we see that
the other terms on the right-hand side of (
2.17
)
also satisfy the same bound.
Hence,
the tail estimate (
2.22
)
follows
from (
2.23
) and Chebyshev’s inequality
(see the proof of
[
2
, Lemma 3]
4
4

4

Lemma 2.1 in the arXiv version.
); see also
[
58
, Lemma 4.5]
.

(iii) A straightforward modification yields the corresponding results for
\Phi_{j}
,
and thus we omit details.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

(i) As a consequence of (
2.21
),
we have the following moment bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\|Z^{(\ell)}\|_{C_{J}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}^{p}\Big]\lesssim p^{\frac{\ell}{2}}

for any finite
p\geq 1

and any interval
J\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
|J|=1
,
uniformly in
J
and
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ii)
In Section
5
,
we need to deal with the product
\Psi_{k}\mathbf{P}_{M}^{\perp}\Psi_{j}
with
k\neq j
, where

\mathbf{P}^{\perp}_{M}=\textbf{Id}-\mathbf{P}_{M}
.
It follows from a slight modification of the proof of Lemma
2.5

that
there exists small
\theta>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\Psi_{k}\mathbf{P}_{M}^{\perp}\Psi_{j}\|_{C_{T}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}^{p}\big]\lesssim_{T}pM^{-\theta p}

(2.24)

for any finite
p\geq 1
,
uniformly in
j,k,M\in\mathbb{N}
.

Lastly, we state a variant of
[
23
, Lemma 2.4]

and its consequence (see
[
23
, Subsection 3.2]
)
on the
logarithmically divergent nature of the stochastic convolution
\Psi_{j}

in (
2.10
).

Lemma 2.7
.

Given
0<s<1
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
, let
I=I_{s,M}

be the
I
-operator defined in (
2.7
).
Given
j\in\mathbb{N}
and

(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
,

I\Psi_{j}(t,x)
is a mean-zero Gaussian random variable,
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(I\Psi_{j}(t,x))^{2}\big]\lesssim_{s}\log\langle M\rangle,

(2.25)

uniformly in

j\in\mathbb{N}
and

(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
Furthermore, given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
a random variable
R_{N}

and
\theta=\theta(s)\gg 1
, independent of
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}[R_{N}]<\infty

(2.26)

and

\displaystyle\|I\Psi_{j}\|_{L^{r}_{[t_{1},t_{2}],x}\mathcal{A}_{N}}\leq re^{\frac{1}{r}\theta T\log\langle M\rangle}R_{N}^{\frac{1}{r}}

(2.27)

for any

1\leq r<\infty
,

0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq T
,
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
where

L^{r}_{[t_{1},t_{2}],x}\mathcal{A}_{N}=L^{r}_{[t_{1},t_{2}]}L^{r}_{x}\mathcal{A}_{N}
.

Proof.

The bound (
2.25
)
follows
from a straightforward modification of the proof of

[
23
, Lemma 2.4]
and thus we omit details.

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
define
R_{N}=R_{N}(\theta)
by setting

\displaystyle R_{N}=\sum_{M=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}e^{-\theta\ell\log\langle M\rangle}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}e^{\|I\Psi_{j}(t,x)\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}}}dxdt,

(2.28)

where
\theta=\theta(s)\gg 1
(to be chosen later) and
\mathcal{A}_{N}=\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathbb{R}
is as in (
1.11
).
Then, from (
2.28
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|I\Psi_{j}\|_{L^{r}_{[t_{1},t_{2}],x}\mathcal{A}_{N}}^{r}&=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\|I\Psi_{j}(t,x)\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}}^{r}dxdt\leq r!\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}e^{\|I_{N}\Psi_{j}(t,x)\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}}}dxdt\\
&\leq r!e^{\theta T\log\langle M\rangle}R_{N}\end{split}

(2.29)

for any

1\leq r<\infty
,

0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq T
,
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
By noting
r!\leq r^{r}
,
the bound (
2.27
) follows from (
2.29
).

It remains to prove (
2.26
).
Note that the function
e^{(1+|x|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}
is convex.
From (
1.11
)
and Jensen’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle e^{\|I\Psi_{j}(t,x)\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigg(\Big\{1+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big(I\Psi_{j}(t,x)\big)^{2}\Big\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}e^{1+|I\Psi_{j}(t,x)|}.

Then, from (
2.28
), (
2.25
),
and the fact that

I\Psi_{j}(t,x)
is a mean-zero Gaussian random variable,
satisfying (
2.25
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[R_{N}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{M=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}e^{-\theta\ell\log\langle M\rangle}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{|I\Psi_{j}(t,x)|}\Big]dxdt

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{M=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}e^{-\theta\ell\log\langle M\rangle}\cdot\ell e^{C_{s}\log\langle M\rangle}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
where the last step follows
from taking
sufficiently large

\theta=\theta(s)\gg 1
.
This proves (
2.26
).
∎

3.
Well-posedness of the hyperbolic
O(N)
linear sigma model

In this section, we prove pathwise global
well-posedness of

\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.10
)
(Theorem
1.1
).
In fact, by the first order expansion (
1.7
), we show global well-posedness of the SdNLW
system (
1.8
) for

\mathbf{v}_{N}=\{v_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
, which we write out below for readers’ convenience:

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)v_{N,j}&=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{N,k}^{2}v_{N,j}+2\Psi_{k}v_{N,k}v_{N,j}+v_{N,k}^{2}\Psi_{j}\\
&\hphantom{XXXXX}+:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}}\!:v_{N,j}+2v_{N,k}:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:+:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}\Psi_{j}}\!:\Big)\end{split}

(3.1)

for
j=1,\dots,N
.

In Subsection
3.1
, we prove local well-posedness of (
3.1
) via a standard
contraction argument.
In Subsection
3.2
, we then establish some preliminary uniform
(in
N
) estimates for the modified energy

E_{N}(t)

defined in (
3.15
).
Finally, in Subsection
3.3
,
we prove global well-posedness of (
3.1
),
with uniform (in
N
) bounds,
by adapting
the hybrid
I
-method introduced in
[
23
]

to the current vector-valued setting.
We note that a slight modification of the argument presented in this section also yields
pathwise global well-posedness for (
1.42
)
(Theorem
1.6
(i)).
We omit details.

3.1.
Local well-posedness

In this subsection, we prove local well-posedness
of (
3.1
).
In order to separate the deterministic and probabilistic components
of the argument, we
consider the following system
of SdNLW
with a given
deterministic

enhanced data set

\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}=\big\{Z_{j}^{(1)},Z_{k,j}^{(2)},Z_{k,j}^{(3)}\big\}_{j,k=1}^{N}

as in
(
2.16
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{t}+m-\Delta)v_{N,j}&=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{N,k}^{2}v_{N,j}+2Z_{k}^{(1)}v_{N,k}v_{N,j}+v_{N,k}^{2}Z_{j}^{(1)}\\
&\hphantom{XXXXX}+Z_{k,k}^{(2)}v_{N,j}+2v_{N,k}Z_{k,j}^{(2)}+Z_{k,j}^{(3)}\Big)\end{split}

(3.2)

for
j=1,\dots,N
.
Under (
2.19
),
the system (
3.2
) reduces to (
3.1
),
while it reduces to (
1.41
)
under (
2.20
).

Given an interval
J\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
define the
X^{s}_{N}(J)
-norm by setting

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{\mathbf{v}}_{N}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}&=\|(\mathbf{v}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{N})\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\\
&=\|(v_{N,j},\partial_{t}v_{N,j})\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}.\end{split}

(3.3)

When
J=[0,\tau]
,
with a slight abuse of notation,
we set

X^{s}_{N}(\tau)=X^{s}_{N}([0,\tau])
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
m>0
and
\frac{1}{2}\leq s<1

and
T\geq 1
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
suppose that

\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}=\big\{Z_{j}^{(1)},Z_{k,j}^{(2)},Z_{k,j}^{(3)}\big\}_{j,k=1}^{N}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}_{N}(T),

and

\big(\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(0)},\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(1)}\big)=\big\{\big(v^{(0)}_{N,j},v^{(1)}_{N,j}\big)\big\}_{j=1}^{N}\in\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,
where

\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}_{N}(T)

and

\mathcal{A}_{N}B

are as in (
2.17
)
and (
1.11
), respectively.
Then, given any
t_{0}\in[0,T)
,
there exists a
unique solution
\vec{\mathbf{v}}_{N}=(\mathbf{v}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{N})=\{(v_{N,j},\partial_{t}v_{N,j})\}_{j=1}^{N}

to (
3.2
)
with

\vec{\mathbf{v}}_{N}|_{t=t_{0}}=\big(\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(0)},\mathbf{v}_{N}^{(1)}\big)

on the time interval

[t_{0},t_{0}+\tau]\cap[0,T]
,
where the local existence time

\tau=\tau\Big(\big\|\big(v^{(0)}_{N,j},v^{(1)}_{N,j}\big)\big\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}},\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}\|_{\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(T)}\Big)>0

does not depend on
N\in\mathbb{N}
in an explicit manner.
Moreover, the solution
\vec{\mathbf{v}}_{N}

is unique
in the entire class
X^{s}_{N}([t_{0},(t_{0}+\tau)\wedge T])
, where

a\wedge b=\min(a,b)
.

Theorem
1.1
(i)
follows from Proposition
3.1
,
(
2.19
), and Lemma
2.5
.
We omit details.

Remark 3.2
.

(i)
As pointed out in Section
1
,
by using the Strichartz estimates as in
[
21
]
,
it is possible to prove local well-posedness
of (
3.2
) for
s>\frac{1}{4}
.
However, the uniqueness guaranteed
by such an argument holds
only in the intersection of

X^{s}_{N}(\tau\wedge(T-t_{0}))

with a suitable
\ell^{2}
-averaged version
of Strichartz spaces.
A similar remark holds for
the local well-posedness argument
of the mean-field SdNLW (
1.20
) (see Proposition
4.1
)
for which
unconditional uniqueness
of solutions
plays a key role in Section
6
.
Since we prefer to have a uniform treatment
for both
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.10
)
and
the mean-field SdNLW (
1.20
),
we restrict our attention to
\frac{1}{2}\leq s<1

in
Proposition
3.1

(and also in Proposition
4.1
),
guaranteeing
unconditional uniqueness of solutions.

(ii) Let
T\geq 1
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\mathbf{v}_{N}
be the local-in-time solution
to (
3.2
) constructed in Proposition
3.1
.
Then,
by denoting by
T_{*}
its maximal existence time,
the following blowup alternative holds:

\displaystyle T_{*}=T\qquad\text{or}\qquad\lim_{t\to T_{*}-}\|(\mathbf{v}_{N},\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{N})(t)\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}=\infty.

(3.4)

We now present a proof of
Proposition
3.1
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

The claimed local well-posedness of (
3.2
)
follows
from a straightforward adaptation of the proof of

[
23
, Proposition 4.1]
.
For readers’ convenience, however, we present some details,
in particular to show
that
the local existence time
\tau>0
does not depend
on
N\in\mathbb{N}
in an explicit manner.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
t_{0}=0
.
Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
By writing (
3.1
) in the Duhamel formulation,
we have

\displaystyle v_{N,j}(t)=S(t)\big(v^{(0)}_{N,j},v^{(1)}_{N,j}\big)-\sum_{\ell=1}^{6}\mathcal{I}(A_{j,\ell})(t),\quad j=1,\dots,N,

(3.5)

where
S(t)
and
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{0}
are as in (
2.5
) and (
2.6
), respectively,
and

\{A_{j,\ell}=A_{j,\ell}(N)\}_{j=1}^{N}
,
\ell=1,\dots,6
, are given by

\displaystyle A_{j,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}v_{N,k}^{2}v_{N,j},\qquad A_{j,2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}2Z_{k}^{(1)}v_{N,k}v_{N,j},

\displaystyle A_{j,3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}v_{N,k}^{2}Z_{j}^{(1)},\qquad A_{j,4}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}Z_{k,k}^{(2)}v_{N,j},

\displaystyle A_{j,5}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}2v_{N,k}Z_{k,j}^{(2)},\qquad A_{j,6}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}Z_{k,j}^{(3)}.

Fix
0<\tau\leq 1

and small
\varepsilon>0

such that
s\leq 1-\varepsilon
.
By
Sobolev’s and Hölder’s inequalities
with (
3.3
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{\mathcal{I}}(A_{j,1})\|_{X^{s}_{N,j}(\tau)}&\lesssim\tau\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|v_{N,k}^{2}v_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{\tau}H_{x}^{s-1}}\\
&\lesssim\tau\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|v_{N,k}\|_{C_{\tau}L^{\frac{6}{2-s}}_{x}}^{2}\|v_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{\tau}L^{\frac{6}{2-s}}_{x}}\\
&\leq\tau\|\vec{\mathbf{v}}_{N}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(\tau)}^{3},\end{split}

(3.6)

provided that
\frac{1}{2}\leq s
(
<1
).
Here,
\vec{\mathcal{I}}(A_{j,1})
is given by

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{I}}(A_{j,1})=\big(\mathcal{I}(A_{j,1}),\partial_{t}\mathcal{I}(A_{j,1})\big)

(3.7)

and we use analogous notations in the following.
By (
3.3
),
Sobolev’s inequality,
Lemma
2.1
(ii),
the fractional Leibniz rule
(Lemma
2.1
(i)),
Sobolev’s inequality,
and Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality (in
k
) with (
2.17
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{\mathcal{I}}(A_{j,2})\|_{X^{s}_{N,j}(\tau)}&\lesssim\tau\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|Z_{k}^{(1)}v_{N,k}v_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{\tau}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\frac{2}{2-s-\varepsilon}}}\\
&\lesssim\tau\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|Z_{k}^{(1)}\|_{C_{T}W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}_{x}}\|v_{N,k}v_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{\tau}W_{x}^{\varepsilon,\frac{2}{2-s-\varepsilon}}}\\
&\lesssim\tau\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|Z_{k}^{(1)}\|_{C_{T}W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}_{x}}\|v_{N,k}\|_{C_{\tau}H^{\frac{s}{2}+\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}_{x}}\|v_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{\tau}H^{\frac{s}{2}+\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}_{x}}\\
&\leq\tau\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}\|_{\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(T)}\|\vec{\mathbf{v}}_{N}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(\tau)}^{2},\end{split}

(3.8)

provided that
3\varepsilon\leq s\leq 1-\varepsilon
.
A similar computation yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{\mathcal{I}}(A_{j,3})\|_{X^{s}_{N,j}(\tau)}\lesssim\tau\|\boldsymbol{Z}\|_{\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(T)}\|\vec{\mathbf{v}}_{N}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(\tau)}^{2}.\end{split}

(3.9)

By Sobolev’s inequality,
Lemma
2.1
(ii),
and (
2.1
)
with (
2.17
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{\mathcal{I}}(A_{j,4})\|_{X^{s}_{N,j}(\tau)}&\lesssim\tau\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|Z_{k,k}^{(2)}v_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{\tau}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\frac{2}{2-s-\varepsilon}}}\\
&\lesssim\tau\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|Z_{k,k}^{(2)}\|_{C_{T}W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}_{x}}\|v_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{\tau}W_{x}^{\varepsilon,\frac{2}{2-s-\varepsilon}}}\\
&\lesssim\tau\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}\|_{\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(T)}\|\vec{\mathbf{v}}_{N}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(\tau)},\end{split}

(3.10)

provided that
\varepsilon\leq s\leq 1-\varepsilon
.
Similarly, by Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality (in
k
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{\mathcal{I}}(A_{j,5})\|_{X^{s}_{N,j}(\tau)}&\lesssim\tau\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|v_{N,k}Z_{k,j}^{(2)}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{\tau}H_{x}^{s-1}}\\
&\lesssim\tau\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}\|_{\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(T)}\|\vec{\mathbf{v}}_{N}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(\tau)}.\end{split}

(3.11)

Lastly,
it follows from
(
3.3
),
(
2.1
),
and (
2.2
)
with
(
2.17
) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{\mathcal{I}}(A_{j,6})\|_{X^{s}_{N,j}(\tau)}&\lesssim\tau\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}Z_{k,j}^{(3)}\bigg\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}C_{T}W_{x}^{s-1,\infty}}\leq\tau\|Z_{k,j}^{(3)}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}^{(2)}C_{T}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}\\
&\leq\tau\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}\|_{\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(T)},\end{split}

(3.12)

provided that
s-1\leq-\varepsilon
.

Denote the right-hand side of (
3.5
)
by
