\displaystyle\bigg(\Big[\frac{T}{\tau}\Big]+1\bigg)\bigg((2C_{1})^{[\frac{T}{\tau}]}\eta_{0}+2C_{3}\frac{(2C_{1})^{[\frac{T}{\tau}]}}{2C_{1}-1}\eta\bigg)\leq\delta\ll 1.

(5.65)

Then, it follows from
(
5.64
) and (
5.65
) that

\displaystyle\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{[T/\tau]}\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J_{\ell})}\leq\delta,

on
\Omega_{N}(R,K,\eta_{0},\eta)

defined in (
5.47
),
for any

N\geq\max\big(N_{1}(\eta_{0}(\delta),\delta),N_{2}(\eta(\delta),\delta)\big)
,
where
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are as in
(
5.40
) and (
5.45
), respectively.
In view of
(
5.48
),
this proves
the
convergence-in-probability
(
5.35
).

\bullet

Step 2:

In this part, we establish the convergence
(
1.26
)
for each
fixed

j\in\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}_{x}^{s}}=\|\vec{v}_{N,j}-\vec{v}_{j}\|_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}_{x}^{s}}\longrightarrow 0

(5.66)

in probability
as
N\to\infty
.

We first go over the construction of data sets indexed by
N\gg 1
.
Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Given
R\geq 1
, define
the set
\Omega_{5,j}(R)\subset\Omega

by setting

\displaystyle\Omega_{5,j}(R)=\Big\{\omega\in\Omega:\|\vec{v}_{j}\|_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\leq R\Big\}.

(5.67)

Then, it follows from Chebyshev’s inequality
and (
5.36
) that
there exists
R=R(\delta)\gg 1
, independent of
j\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big((\Omega_{5,j}(R))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{10}.

(5.68)

Given small
\eta_{0}>0
,
define
\Omega_{6,j,N}(\eta_{0})\subset\Omega
by setting

\displaystyle\Omega_{6,j,N}(\eta_{0})=\Big\{\omega\in\Omega:\|\vec{V}_{N,j}(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\leq\eta_{0}\Big\}.

(5.69)

Then, from
the assumption (
1.24
) on the initial data,
there exists
N_{3}=N_{3}(j,\eta_{0},\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big((\Omega_{6,j,N}(\eta_{0}))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{10}.

(5.70)

for any
N\geq N_{3}
.
Given small
\eta>0
,
define the
\Omega_{7,N}(\eta)\subset\Omega

by setting

\displaystyle\Omega_{7,N}(\eta)=\Big\{\omega\in\Omega:\|\vec{V}_{N,k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}\leq\eta\Big\}

(5.71)

Then, it follows
from the convergence-in-probability (
5.35
)
established in
Step 1 that, given
\delta>0
,
there exists
N_{4}=N_{4}(\eta,\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big((\Omega_{7,N}(\eta))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{10}

(5.72)

for any
N\geq N_{4}
.
Given
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
K\geq 1
, define the set
\Omega_{8,j,N}(K)\subset\Omega
by setting

\displaystyle\Omega_{8,j,N}(K)=\Big\{\omega\in\Omega:\|:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,k}C_{T}W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}_{x}}\leq K\Big\}.

(5.73)

Then, it follows from Lemma
2.5
(i)
(for the uniform (in
j,k
) moment bound
on the
C_{T}W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}_{x}
-norm of
:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:
)
and Chebyshev’s inequality
that
there exists
K=K(\delta)\gg 1
, independent of
j,N\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big((\Omega_{8,j,N}(K))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{10}.

(5.74)

Given small
\eta>0
,
define the set

\Omega_{9,j,N}(\eta)\subset\Omega
by setting

\displaystyle\Omega_{9,j,N}(\eta)=\Big\{\omega\in\Omega:\|(\mathbf{v},\boldsymbol{\Psi})\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{j,N}}\leq\eta\Big\},

(5.75)

where the
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{j,N}
-norm is defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|(\mathbf{v},\boldsymbol{\Psi})\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{j,N}}&=\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}\Psi_{j}}\!:\bigg\|_{L_{T}^{2}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}\\
&\quad+\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{k}\!:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}\Psi_{k}]\Psi_{j}\Big)\bigg\|_{L_{T}^{2}H_{x}^{s-1}}.\end{split}

(5.76)

Then,
it follows from Lemmas
5.1
and
5.4

that there exists
N_{5}=N_{5}(j,\eta,\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big((\Omega_{9,j,N}(\eta))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{10}

(5.77)

for
N\geq N_{5}
.

We now turn to estimating the terms
on the right-hand side of (
5.33
)
for
fixed

j\in\mathbb{N}
,
which follows from
a straightforward modification of the estimates in Step 1.
In the following, we work on

\Omega_{j,N}(R,K,\eta_{0},\eta)
defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega_{j,N}(R,K,\eta_{0},\eta)&=\Omega_{N}(R,K,\eta_{0},\eta)\cap\Omega_{5,j}(R)\cap\Omega_{6,j,N}(\eta_{0})\\
&\quad\cap\Omega_{7,N}(\eta)\cap\Omega_{8,j,N}(K)\cap\Omega_{9,j,N}(\eta).\end{split}

(5.78)

where

\Omega_{N}(R,K,\eta_{0},\eta)
,

\Omega_{5,j}(R)
,

\Omega_{6,j,N}(\eta_{0})
,

\Omega_{7,N}(\eta)
,
\Omega_{8,j,N}(K)
, and
\Omega_{9,j,N}(\eta)

are
as in (
5.47
),
(
5.67
),
(
5.69
),
(
5.71
), (
5.73
), and (
5.75
),
respectively.
Then, given small
\delta>0
,
by choosing

R=R(\delta)

and
K=K(\delta)
sufficiently large,
it follows from (
5.78
)
with (
5.48
),
(
5.68
), (
5.70
),
(
5.72
), (
5.74
), and (
5.77
)
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big((\Omega_{j,N}(R,K,\eta))^{c}\big)<\delta

(5.79)

for any
N\geq N_{*}
, where

\displaystyle N_{*}=\max\Big(N_{1}(\eta_{0},\delta),N_{2}(\eta,\delta),N_{3}(j,\eta_{0},\delta),N_{4}(\eta,\delta),N_{5}(j,\eta,\delta)\big)\Big).

(5.80)

Here,
N_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,\dots,5
,
are as in
(
5.40
)
(
5.45
),
(
5.70
),
(
5.72
),
and
(
5.77
),
respectively.

Let
J\subset[0,T]
be an interval
with
|J|\leq 1
.
Proceeding as in (
5.50
)
with
(
5.49
),
(
5.37
),
(
5.43
), (
5.44
),
(
5.67
),
and
(
5.71
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A}_{j,1})\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}&\lesssim|J|\Big(\|\vec{V}_{N,k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}^{2}+\|\vec{v}_{k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}^{2}\Big)\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\\
&\quad+|J|\|\vec{V}_{N,k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}\Big(\|\vec{V}_{N,k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}+\|\vec{v}_{k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}\Big)\|\vec{v}_{j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\\
&\quad+|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{k}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}^{2}]\Big)\bigg\|_{L_{J}^{2}L^{\frac{3}{2-s}}_{x}}\|\vec{v}_{j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim|J|R^{2}\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}+|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta R^{2},\end{split}

(5.81)

provided that
\frac{1}{2}\leq s
(
<1
).
Proceeding as in (
5.52
)
with
(
5.51
),
(
5.37
),
(
5.41
),
(
5.43
),
(
5.44
),
(
5.67
),
and
(
5.71
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|&\vec{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A}_{j,2})\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim|J|\|\Psi_{k}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}C_{J}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}\Big(\|\vec{V}_{N,k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}+\|\vec{v}_{k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}\Big)\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\\
&\quad+|J|\|\Psi_{k}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}C_{J}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}\|\vec{V}_{N,k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}\|\vec{v}_{j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\\
&\quad+|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(\Psi_{k}v_{k}-\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{k}v_{k}]\Big)\bigg\|_{L^{2}_{J}W^{-\varepsilon,\frac{4}{2-s-\varepsilon}}_{x}}\|\vec{v}_{j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim|J|KR\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}+|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta KR,\end{split}

(5.82)

provided that
3\varepsilon\leq s
(
<1
).
Similarly,
proceeding as in (
5.54
)
with
(
5.53
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|&\vec{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A}_{j,3})\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim|J|\Big(\|\vec{V}_{N,k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}+\|\vec{v}_{k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}\Big)\|\vec{V}_{N,k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}\|\Psi_{j}\|_{C_{J}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}\\
&\quad+|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{k}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}^{2}]\Big)\bigg\|_{L_{J}^{2}W_{x}^{\varepsilon,\frac{2}{2-s-\varepsilon}}}\|\Psi_{j}\|_{C_{J}W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta KR.\end{split}

(5.83)

Proceeding as in (
5.55
)
with (
5.34
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A}_{j,4})\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}&\lesssim|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}}\!:\bigg\|_{L^{2}_{T}W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}_{x}}\Big(\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}+\|\vec{v}_{j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\Big)\\
&\lesssim|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}+|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta R,\end{split}

(5.84)

provided that
\varepsilon\leq s\leq 1-\varepsilon
.
Proceeding as in (
5.57
) with (
5.56
)
and (
5.73
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A}_{j,5})\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}&\lesssim|J|\|:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,k}C_{J}W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}_{x}}\|\vec{V}_{N,k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}+|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta\\
&\lesssim|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta K.\end{split}

(5.85)

Lastly, from (
5.34
)
and
(
5.75
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A}_{j,6})\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}&\lesssim|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}:\!{\Psi_{k}^{2}\Psi_{j}}\!:\bigg\|_{L^{2}_{J}H^{s-1}_{x}}\\
&\leq|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta.\end{split}

(5.86)

Therefore,
putting (
5.33
)
(but starting at time
t_{0}
),
(
5.81
),
(
5.82
),
(
5.83
),
(
5.84
),
(
5.85
),
and (
5.86
)
together
with
J=[t_{0},t_{1}]\subset[0,T]

such that
|J|\leq 1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}&\leq C_{4}\|\vec{V}_{N,j}(t_{0})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}+C_{5}|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}R(K+R)\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\\
&\quad+C_{6}|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta R(K+R).\end{split}

(5.87)

Suppose that

\displaystyle\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\leq 1

for some interval
J\subset[0,T]
.
Then,
it follows from (
5.87
) that
there exists
\tau=\tau(R,K)\in(0,1]
such that

\displaystyle\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{J}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{4}\|\vec{V}_{N,j}(t_{0})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}+2C_{6}\eta,

provided that
|J|\leq\tau
.
Then,
by recalling that
\tau
depends only on
\delta

and by choosing

\eta_{0}=\eta_{0}(\delta)
and
\eta=\eta(\delta)
sufficiently small,
it follows from
a slight modification of the iterative argument in Step 1
with
(
5.69
)
that

\displaystyle\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\leq\delta

on
\Omega_{j,N}(R,K,\eta_{0},\eta)
,
satisfying
(
5.79
),
for any

N\geq N_{*}
,
where

N_{*}
is as in
(
5.80
).
This proves
the
convergence-in-probability
(
5.66
),
and therefore
concludes the proof of Theorem
1.4
(i).

5.3.
On the convergence rate
under a higher moment assumption

In this subsection,
we present a proof of Theorem
1.4
(ii)
on a convergence rate under the higher moment assumption (
1.30
):

\displaystyle v_{j}\in L^{6}(\Omega;C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))),\qquad j\in\mathbb{N}

(5.88)

for some
T>0
and
\frac{1}{2}\leq s<1
.
Given
\big(v_{j}^{(0)},v_{j}^{(1)}\big)\in L^{6}(\Omega;\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
,
Proposition
4.1

guarantees (
5.88
)
only for a short time.
As in Subsection
5.2
, however,
the convergence argument presented below
works on
[0,T]
for any given
T>0
,

assuming
that (
5.88
)
(together with (
1.28
) and (
1.29
)) holds.

As compared to the presentation
in Subsection
5.2
,
the only difference appears on
how we handle the terms in (
5.44
)
and (
5.76
).
More precisely,
we show that the terms in
(
5.44
)
and (
5.76
)
converge in probability to 0
with a rate
N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{0}}
for any
\varepsilon_{0}>0
.
In view of
Lemma
5.1
which comes with a convergence rate
of
N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{0}}
,
we only need to establish
suitable replacements
of
Lemmas
5.2
and
5.4
.

The following lemma establishes an extension of
Lemma
5.4
under the higher moment assumption on
v_{j}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\frac{1}{2}\leq s<1

and
\{(\Psi_{j},v_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be as in Lemma
5.2
.
Moreover, assume that

\displaystyle v_{j}\in L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})))

(5.89)

for some
p>2
.
Then, given
T,\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
we have, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{k}\!:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}\Psi_{k}]\Psi_{j}\Big)\bigg\|_{L_{T}^{2}H_{x}^{s-1}}\longrightarrow 0

(5.90)

in probability as
N\to\infty
,
and

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{k}\!:\!{\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}}\!:-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}\Psi_{k}]\Psi_{j}\Big)\bigg\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}L_{T}^{2}H_{x}^{s-1}}\longrightarrow 0

(5.91)

in probability as
N\to\infty
.

Proof.

We only consider (
5.91
) since (
5.90
) follows
from a similar consideration.
From a slight modification of the proof of Lemma
5.4
,
it is easy to see that
the contribution from the case
k=j

satisfies (
5.91
).
Thus, we only consider the case
k\neq j
in the following.

Set

\displaystyle X_{k,j}=v_{k}\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}\Psi_{k}]\Psi_{j}

Then,
from the mutual independence of
\{(\Psi_{k},v_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X_{k,j}]=\mathbb{E}\big[v_{k}\Psi_{k}-\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{k}v_{k}]\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{j}]=0

(5.92)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X_{k,j}\cdot X_{k^{\prime},j}]=\mathbb{E}\big[(v_{k}\Psi_{k}-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}\Psi_{k}])(v_{k^{\prime}}\Psi_{k^{\prime}}-\mathbb{E}[v_{k^{\prime}}\Psi_{k^{\prime}}])\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{j}^{2}]=0

(5.93)

for any
k\neq k^{\prime}
with
k,k^{\prime}\neq j
.

A slight modification of (
5.14
)
with (
5.89
) yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Big\|\|v_{k}\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}\|_{L_{T}^{2}H_{x}^{s-1}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}&\lesssim_{T}\|v_{k}\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}C_{T}H_{x}^{s}}\|\Psi_{k}\Psi_{j}\|_{L^{\frac{2p}{p-2}}_{\omega}C_{T}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}\\
&\lesssim 1,\end{split}

(5.94)

uniformly in
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Similarly,
a minor modification of (
5.15
) yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Big\|\|\mathbb{E}[v_{k}\Psi_{k}]\Psi_{j}\|_{L_{T}^{2}H_{x}^{s-1}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}&\lesssim_{T}1,\end{split}

(5.95)

uniformly in
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence, proceeding as in (
5.7
) with
(
5.92
), (
5.93
),
(
5.94
), and (
5.95
),
we have

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N}X_{k,j}\bigg\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}\mathcal{A}_{N,j}L_{T}^{2}H_{x}^{s-1}}\leq\frac{1}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|X_{k,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}^{(2)}L^{2}_{\omega}L_{T}^{2}H_{x}^{s-1}}\lesssim_{T}\frac{1}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}},

(5.96)

where
\mathcal{A}_{N}^{(2)}B
is as in (
2.2
).
Therefore, the convergence-in-probability (
5.91
) follows from
Chebyshev’s inequality with (
5.96
).
∎

In the proof of Lemma
5.5
,
we crucially used the fact that the space-time norm
is
L^{2}
-based.
Since the terms treated in Lemma
5.2

involve the
L^{r}_{x}
-base Sobolev spaces with
r>2
,
we can not proceed as in the proof of
Lemma
5.5
.
We instead make the observation that
the terms
treated in Lemma
5.2

only appear in
(
5.49
), (
5.51
),
and (
5.53
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{k}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}^{2}]\Big)v_{j},\qquad\frac{2}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(\Psi_{k}v_{k}-\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{k}v_{k}]\Big)v_{j}\\
&\text{and}\qquad\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{k}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}^{2}]\Big)\Psi_{j}\end{split}

(5.97)

which do not involve
the difference
V_{N,j}
defined in (
5.32
).
The following lemma establishes
convergence in probability of these terms
in a direct manner.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\frac{1}{2}\leq s<1

and
T>0
.
Let
\{(\Psi_{j},v_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be as in Lemma
5.2
.
Moreover, assume (
5.88
)
:

\displaystyle v_{j}\in L^{6}(\Omega;C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))).

(5.98)

Then, given
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
we have, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(\Psi_{k}v_{k}-\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{k}v_{k}]\Big)v_{j}\bigg\|_{L_{T}^{2}H^{s-1}_{x}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0,

(5.99)

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{k}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}^{2}]\Big)v_{j}\bigg\|_{L_{T}^{2}H^{s-1}_{x}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0,

(5.100)

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{k}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}^{2}]\Big)\Psi_{j}\bigg\|_{L_{T}^{2}H^{s-1}_{x}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0,

(5.101)

in probability, as
N\to\infty
,
and

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(\Psi_{k}v_{k}-\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{k}v_{k}]\Big)v_{j}\bigg\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}L_{T}^{2}H^{s-1}_{x}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0,

(5.102)

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{k}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}^{2}]\Big)v_{j}\bigg\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}L_{T}^{2}H^{s-1}_{x}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0,

(5.103)

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big(v_{k}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}^{2}]\Big)\Psi_{j}\bigg\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}L_{T}^{2}H^{s-1}_{x}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0,

(5.104)

in probability, as
N\to\infty
.

Proof.

Since the claim follows from a slight modification of the proof of Lemma
5.5
,
we keep the discussion brief.
We only consider (
5.103
) since (
5.99
), (
5.100
),
(
5.101
), (
5.102
),
and (
5.104
) follow
from a similar consideration.
We first note that, by arguing as in
the proof of Lemma
5.4
,
the contributions from the case
k=j

can be treated easily and thus we omit details.
In the following,
we assume
k\neq j
.

Set

\displaystyle Y_{k,j}=\Big(v_{k}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[v_{k}^{2}]\Big)v_{j}

(5.105)

Then,
from the mutual independence of
\{(\Psi_{k},v_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y_{k,j}]=\mathbb{E}[Y_{k,j}\cdot Y_{k^{\prime},j}]=0

(5.106)

for any
k\neq k^{\prime}
with
k,k^{\prime}\neq j
;
see (
5.92
) and (
5.93
).

Proceeding as in (
3.6
) with (
5.98
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Big\|\|v_{k}^{2}v_{j}\|_{L_{T}^{2}H_{x}^{s-1}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}&\lesssim_{T}\|v_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{6}_{\omega}C_{T}H_{x}^{s}}\|v_{j}\|_{L^{6}_{\omega}C_{T}H_{x}^{s}}\\
&\lesssim 1,\end{split}

(5.107)

uniformly in
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Similarly,
a minor modification of (
5.15
) yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Big\|\|\mathbb{E}[v_{k}^{2}]v_{j}\|_{L_{T}^{2}H_{x}^{s-1}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}&\lesssim 1,\end{split}

(5.108)

uniformly in
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, the claim (
5.103
) follows
from proceeding as in the proof of Lemma
5.5

with (
5.105
), (
5.106
), (
5.107
), and (
5.108
).
We omit details.
∎

With Lemmas
5.5
and

5.6
,
Theorem
1.4
(ii)
follows from
a straightforward modification of the argument presented in Subsection
5.2
.
More precisely, given small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
we need to prove the following two convergence results under the assumption (
5.88
)
together with
the assumed convergence of initial data (
1.28
) and (
1.29
)
with the convergence rate of
N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{0}}
:

(i)

in Step 1, instead of (
5.35
), we
prove

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(T)}=N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,j}-\vec{v}_{j}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(T)}\longrightarrow 0

in probability as
N\to\infty
,

(ii)

in Step 2,
instead (
5.66
),
we prove, for each
fixed

j\in\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{V}_{N,j}\|_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}_{x}^{s}}=N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,j}-\vec{v}_{j}\|_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}_{x}^{s}}\longrightarrow 0

in probability,
as
N\to\infty
.

In Step 1, the only modification
appears in (
5.50
), (
5.52
),
and (
5.54
),
where we treat the terms in (
5.97
)
directly by
Lemmas
5.5
and
5.6
.
Similarly,
in Step 2, the only modification
appears in (
5.81
), (
5.82
),
and (
5.83
),
where we once again treat the terms in (
5.97
)
directly by
Lemmas
5.5
and
5.6
.
Since the required modifications
are straightforward,
we omit details;
see Subsection
6.2

for an example of an argument where we obtain a convergence rate
(in a slightly different setting).
This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.4
(ii).

Remark 5.7
.

Let
T\gg 1
.
In Theorem
1.4
(ii),
we only assume the existence of a solution

\{(u_{j},\partial_{t}u_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

to the mean-field SdNLW (
1.20
)
on the time interval
[0,T]
along with the higher moment assumption
(
1.30
).
In particular,
we did not assume existence
of a solution

\{(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})\}_{j=1}^{N}

to
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.10
)
on the time interval
[0,T]

(whose local-in-time existence
is guaranteed by Theorem
1.1
(i)).
We point out that an iterative application
of the local-in-time convergence argument
together with
the blowup alternative
(
3.4
)
guarantees existence of
the solution
\{(u_{N,j},\partial_{t}u_{N,j})\}_{j=1}^{N}

to
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.10
)
on the time interval
[0,T]

for sufficiently large
N=N(\omega,T)\gg 1
.
See

[
27
]

for an argument,
where
existence
(with a control on the norm)
of a solution to a limiting equation,
say on a time interval
[0,T]
,
guarantees existence
(and a control on the norm)
of converging solutions on the same interval.

6.
Mean-field limit of invariant Gibbs dynamics

In this section,
we present a proof of
Theorem
1.6
(iii)
on convergence of the invariant Gibbs dynamics for

\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.42
)
to the limiting invariant dynamics
for the mean-field SdNLW (
1.43
),
given by (
1.38
).
In Subsection
6.1
,
we go over the construction of the initial data set
on an appropriate probability space
(\Omega^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathbb{P}^{\prime})
.
In Subsection
6.2
,
we then establish convergence of the
invariant Gibbs dynamics for
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.42
).

6.1.
On the Gibbsian initial data

In this subsection,
we go over the construction of the initial data set
stated in
Theorem
1.6
(iii),
which follows from the following proposition.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
m>0
.
Then, there exist a probability space
(\Omega_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{1})
and random variables

\big\{\phi^{(0)}_{j}\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and

\vec{\psi}_{N}=\{\psi_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, on

(\Omega_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{1})
such that
the family
\big\{\phi^{(0)}_{j}\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is independent
and

\displaystyle\Law\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j}\big)=\mu_{1}\ \ \text{for all }j\in\mathbb{N}\quad\text{and}\quad\Law(\vec{\psi}_{N})=\rho_{N},

(6.1)

where
\mu_{1}

is the massive Gaussian free field with mass
m>0

defined in (
1.5
)
and
\rho_{N}

is the
O(N)
linear sigma model defined in (
1.35
).
Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{j=1,\dots,N}\Big\|\big\|\psi_{N,j}-\phi^{(0)}_{j}\big\|_{H^{1}_{x}}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1})}\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.2)

By assuming
Proposition
6.1
,
we first carry out the construction of
the initial data sets stated in Theorem
1.6
(iii.a) and (iii.b).

By extending the probability space

(\Omega_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{1})
constructed in Proposition
6.1
,
let
\{\phi^{(1)}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family of independent random variables
on
\Omega_{1}

such that

\Law\big(\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)=\mu_{0}
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
where
\mu_{0}
is the white noise measure on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
defined in (
1.33
)
with
s=0
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we set

\vec{\phi}^{(0)}_{N}=\{\phi^{(0)}_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}\{\phi^{(0)}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\vec{\phi}^{(1)}_{N}=\{\phi^{(1)}_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}\{\phi^{(1)}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N},

where
\{\phi^{(0)}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is as in Proposition
6.1
.
For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
\vec{v}^{(0)}_{N}=\{v^{(0)}_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
by
setting

\displaystyle v^{(0)}_{N,j}=\psi_{N,j}-\phi^{(0)}_{N,j}=\psi_{N,j}-\phi^{(0)}_{j},\qquad j=1,\dots,N,

(6.3)

where
\vec{\psi}_{N}=\{\psi_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}

is as in Proposition
6.1
.
Then, from (
6.2
), we have

\displaystyle\sup_{j=1,\dots,N}\Big\|\big\|v^{(0)}_{N,j}\big\|_{H^{1}_{x}}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1})}\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}},

yielding (
1.47
).

Let
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

be a family of independent space-time white noise
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}

which is also independent
from

\{\phi^{(0)}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
,

\{\phi^{(1)}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
,
and
\{\vec{\psi}_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Namely, we may assume that

\xi_{j}

is a
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})
-valued random variable
defined on another probability space

(\Omega_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathbb{P}_{2})
.
Then,
by setting
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}=\mathbb{P}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{2}

and
\Omega^{\prime}=\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}
,
we complete
the construction of
the initial data sets stated in Theorem
1.6
(iii).

We now turn to a proof of Proposition
6.1
.
Let us first introduce some notations
and preliminary lemmas.
Let
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
be two probability measures on a Banach space
B
and let
H\subset B
be a closed subspace. We define the Wasserstein-2 distance of
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
with respect to
H

by setting

\displaystyle\Big(W_{H}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\Big)^{2}=\inf_{X\sim\nu_{1},Y\sim\nu_{2}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\|X-Y\|_{H}^{2}\Big],

(6.4)

where
X\sim\nu_{1}
and
Y\sim\nu_{2}
mean that
X
and
Y
are
B
-valued random variables
with
\Law(X)=\nu_{1}
and
\Law(Y)=\nu_{2}
,
and the expectation is taken with respect
to the probability measure associated with the coupling of
X
and
Y
.
Equivalently, it can be written as

\displaystyle\Big(W_{H}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\Big)^{2}=\inf_{\mathfrak{p}\in\Pi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\int_{B\times B}\|x-y\|_{H}^{2}\,d\mathfrak{p}(x,y),

(6.5)

where
\Pi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
is the set of probability measures
\mathfrak{p}

on
B\times B

whose first and second marginals are given by
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
,
namely,

\displaystyle\int_{y\in B}d\mathfrak{p}(x,y)=d\nu_{1}(x)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\int_{x\in B}d\mathfrak{p}(x,y)=d\nu_{2}(y).

(6.6)

See, for example,

[
17
,
16
]
,
where
they take
(B,H,\mu)
to be an abstract Wiener space (for a Gaussian measure

\mu
with its Cameron-Martin space given by
H
)
and
\nu_{j}
,
j=1,2
, to be probability measures on
B
.
In this case, the infimum in (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) is indeed attained.

In our setting, we take

B=\big(H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}

and consider two probability measures:
the Gaussian measure
\mu_{1}^{\otimes N}
, where
\mu_{1}

is the massive Gaussian free field with mass
m>0

defined in (
1.5
),
and
the coupled
\Phi^{4}_{2}
-measure

\rho_{N}
in (
1.35
).
Then, we take

H
to be its Cameron-Martin space
of
\mu_{1}^{\otimes N}
,
namely,

H=\big(H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}

endowed with the norm:

\displaystyle\|\vec{f}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})^{\otimes N}}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\|f_{j}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{2}

for
\vec{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{N})
.

We now state a crucial lemma
from a recent work by
Delgadino and Smith

[
14
]
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
m>0
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mu_{1}^{\otimes N}
and
\rho_{N}
be as above.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}W_{(H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\otimes N}}\big(\mu_{1}^{\otimes N},\rho_{N}\big)<\infty.

Lemma
6.2
plays a key role
in studying
the limiting behavior of the coupled
\Phi^{4}_{2}
-measure

\rho_{N}
in (
1.35
)
as
N\to\infty
.
It was first proven by Shen, Smith, Zhu, and Zhu

[
47
, Theorem 5.11; see also (5.14)]

for large masses
m\gg 1
.
In a recent work
[
14
]
,
Delgadino and Smith
extended this result to
arbitrarily small masses
m>0

by making use of
Talagrand’s inequality

[
54
,
44
,
17
]
.

It follows from
Lemma
6.2

with (
6.4
)
that, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist
random vectors

\vec{\phi}_{N}=\{\phi_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}\sim\mu_{1}^{\otimes N}

and
\vec{\psi}_{N}=\{\psi_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}\sim\rho_{N}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\|\vec{\phi}_{N}-\vec{\psi}_{N}\|_{(H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\otimes N}}^{2}\Big]\lesssim 1,

(6.7)

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
We point out that
Proposition
6.1

does not immediately follow
from (
6.7
)
since
\vec{\phi}_{N}
depends on
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
In order to amend this issue, we need the following gluing lemma, which is an
infinite-dimensional generalization of
[
59
, Lemma 7.6]
. Given a measurable space
E
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}(E)
the set of probability measures on
E
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
I
be an index set. Let
E
and
E_{j}
,
j\in I
, be Polish spaces.
Given

\mu\in\mathcal{P}(E)
,
suppose that for each
j\in I
,

\nu_{j}
is a probability measure on
E\times E_{j}
with marginal
\mu
on
E

in the sense of (
6.6
).
Then, there exists a probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}\big(E\times\prod_{j\in I}E_{j}\big)
with marginal
\nu_{j}
on
E\times E_{j}
for each
j\in I
.

Proof.

Let
J\subset I
be a finite set. By the disintegration-of-measure theorem
(
[
19
, Theorem on p. 148]
and
[
18
, Proposition 452O]
), for each
j\in J
, there exists a measurable map
x\in E\mapsto\pi_{j}^{x}\in\mathcal{P}(E_{j})
, uniquely determined
\mu
-almost everywhere, such that

\displaystyle\nu_{j}(A_{j})=\int_{E}\pi_{j}^{x}(A_{j}^{x})d\mu(x)

for any measurable set
A_{j}\subset E\times E_{j}
, where

\displaystyle A_{j}^{x}=\{y\in E_{j}:(x,y)\in A_{j}\}.

We then define
\pi_{J}\in\mathcal{P}(E\times\prod_{j\in J}E_{j})
by setting

\displaystyle\pi_{J}(A_{J})=\int_{E}\Big(\bigotimes_{j\in J}\pi_{j}^{x}\Big)(A_{J}^{x})d\mu(x)

for any measurable set
A_{J}\subset E\times\prod_{j\in J}E_{j}
, where

\displaystyle A_{J}^{x}=\Big\{y\in\prod_{j\in J}E_{j}:(x,y)\in A_{J}\Big\}.

It is not difficult to check that
\pi_{J}
has marginal
\nu_{j}
on
E\times E_{j}
for each
j\in I
.
Furthermore,
one can easily see that the collection of the probability measures
\{\pi_{J}:J\subset I\text{ is finite}\}
is consistent. Then, by the Kolmogorov extension theorem
(
[
1
, Appendix D]
), there exists a measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(E\times\prod_{j\in I}E_{j})
that satisfies the desired properties.
∎

We also need the following lemma; see
[
52
, (v) on p. 52]
.

Lemma 6.4
.

A countable product of Polish spaces is Polish.

We are now ready to present a proof of Proposition
6.1
.

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

As observed above,
it follows from
Lemma
6.2

that, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
there exist

\vec{\phi}_{N}=\{\phi_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}\sim\mu_{1}^{\otimes N}

and
\vec{\psi}_{N}=\{\psi_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}\sim\rho_{N}

such that (
6.7
) holds.
For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we extend

\vec{\phi}_{N}
to
a sequence of independent random variables

\vec{\phi}^{\infty}_{N}=\{\phi_{N,j}^{\infty}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

by setting

\phi_{N,j}^{\infty}=\phi_{N,j}
for
j=1,\dots,N

and
\Law(\phi_{N,j})=\mu_{1}

for
j\geq N+1
.
Then,

\vec{\phi}^{\infty}_{N}

is an
\big(H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
-valued random variable
with the law
\mu_{1}^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
.
Note
from Lemma
6.4

that the space
\big(H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
is a Polish space.

We now apply Lemma
6.3
by taking
I=\mathbb{N}
,

E=\big(H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
,

E_{N}=\big(H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}
,

\mu=\mu_{1}^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
,
and
\nu_{N}=\Law(\vec{\phi}_{N}^{\infty},\vec{\psi}_{N})
for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
By Lemma
6.3
,
there exists
a probability measure
\pi
on
E\times\prod_{N\in\mathbb{N}}E_{N}
with marginal
\nu_{N}
on
E\times E_{N}
for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

The probability measure
\pi
then gives rise to random variables

\vec{\phi}^{(0)}=\big\{\phi^{(0)}_{j}\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
on
E=\big(H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}

and
\vec{\psi}_{N}=\{\psi_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
on
E_{N}=\big(H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\big)^{\otimes N}
for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
satisfying (
6.1
),
such that, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\|\big\{\phi^{(0)}_{j}\big\}_{j=1}^{N}-\vec{\psi}_{N}\big\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})^{\otimes N}}^{2}\Big]<\infty.

(6.8)

It follows from the symmetry in (
1.35
)
that the components
\psi_{N,j}
,
j=1,\dots,N
, have the same distribution.
Hence the desired bound (
6.2
) follows from (
6.8
).
∎

6.2.
Convergence of invariant Gibbs dynamics

In this subsection, we prove convergence
of the invariant Gibbs dynamics for
\text{HLSM}_{N}
(
1.42
)
claimed
in Theorem
1.6
(iii.c).
Since the proof follows closely to that of
a combination of the local well-posedness argument
(the proof of Proposition
3.1
)
presented
in Subsection
3.1

and the iterative argument
presented in Subsection
5.2
,
we keep our discussion brief here,
indicating necessary modifications.

Let

\big\{\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j}\,\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and

\vec{\psi}_{N}=\{\psi_{N,j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
be as in
Theorem
1.6
(iii)
constructed in Subsection
6.1
,
and let

\vec{v}^{(0)}_{N}=\big\{v^{(0)}_{N,j}\big\}_{j=1}^{N}

be as in (
6.3
).
Let
\Phi_{j}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, be the solution to (
1.38
).

By writing (
1.41
) in the Duhamel formulation, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}v_{N,j}&=S(t)\big(v_{N,j}^{(0)},0\big)-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathcal{I}\Big(v_{N,k}^{2}v_{N,j}+2\Phi_{k}v_{N,k}v_{N,j}+v_{N,k}^{2}\Phi_{j}\\
&\hphantom{XXXXXXXXXXX}+:\!{\Phi_{k}^{2}}\!:v_{N,j}+2v_{N,k}:\!{\Phi_{k}\Phi_{j}}\!:+:\!{\Phi_{k}^{2}\Phi_{j}}\!:\Big),\end{split}

(6.9)

where
S(t)
and
\mathcal{I}
are as in (
2.5
) and (
2.6
),
respectively.
Denote by
u_{j}
the limit
of
u_{N,j}=\Phi_{j}+v_{N,j}
.
As mentioned in Subsection
1.3
,
the (formal) limit
u_{j}
solves (
1.43
)
with initial data
\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)
.
Hence, recalling
that

u_{j}\equiv\Phi_{j}

is a solution to (
1.43
)
with
(u_{j},\partial_{t}u_{j})|_{t=0}=\big(\phi^{(0)}_{j},\phi^{(1)}_{j}\big)

and
that the uniqueness
of
(u_{j},\partial_{t}u_{j})
holds in the class (
1.46
),
we conclude that

(u_{j},\partial_{t}u_{j})=(\Phi_{j},\partial_{t}\Phi_{j})
.
Namely, we have
v_{j}=u_{j}-\Phi_{j}\equiv 0
for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
which in particular (and trivially) implies

\displaystyle(v_{j},\partial_{t}v_{j})=(0,0)\in L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})))

for any
p\geq 1

and
T>0
.
This is the main difference and the simplification
as compared to Subsection
5.2
.

\bullet

Step 1:

In this first step, we establish the following convergence
for each
T\gg 1
;
for any
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,j}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(T)}\longrightarrow 0

(6.10)

in probability (with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
) as
N\to\infty
,
where
X^{s}_{N}(T)
is as in (
3.3
).

Fix a target time
T\gg 1
,
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
and small
\delta>0
.
Let
\frac{1}{2}\leq s<1
.
In the following, we suppress dependence
on
T
,
s
,
\varepsilon_{0}
, and small
\varepsilon>0
.
Given small
\eta_{0}>0
, define the set
\Omega^{\prime}_{1,N}(\eta_{0})\subset\Omega^{\prime}

by setting

\displaystyle\Omega^{\prime}_{1,N}(\eta_{0})=\Big\{\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\prime}:N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|v^{(0)}_{N,j}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}H_{x}^{s}}\leq\eta_{0}\Big\}.

(6.11)

where
\mathcal{A}_{N}B
is as in (
1.11
).
Then, it follows from Chebyshev’s inequality
and (
6.2
) with (
6.3
) that
there exists
N_{1}=N_{1}(\eta_{0},\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\big((\Omega^{\prime}_{1,N}(\eta_{0}))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{6}.

(6.12)

for any
N\geq N_{1}
.
Next, given
K\geq 1
define
the sets
\Omega^{\prime}_{2,N}(K)\subset\Omega^{\prime}

by setting

\displaystyle\Omega^{\prime}_{2,N}(K)=\Big\{\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\prime}:\|\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{N}\|_{\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}_{N}(T)}\leq K\Big\},

(6.13)

where
the

\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}_{N}(T)
-norm is as in (
2.17
)
and (
2.18
),
and
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{N}
is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{N}=\big\{\Phi_{j},\,:\!{\Phi_{k}\Phi_{j}}\!:\,,\,\vec{0}_{j,k}\big\}_{j,k=1}^{N}

with
\vec{0}_{j,k}=0
for any
j,k=1,\dots,N
.
Then, it follows from Lemma
2.5
(iii)
that
there exists
K=K(\delta)\gg 1
, independent of
N\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\big((\Omega^{\prime}_{2,N}(K))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{6}.

(6.14)

Given small
\eta>0
, define
the set
\Omega^{\prime}_{3,N}(\eta)\subset\Omega^{\prime}

by setting

\displaystyle\Omega^{\prime}_{3,N}(\eta)\subset\Omega^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Bigg\{\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\prime}:N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}:\!{\Phi_{k}^{2}\Phi_{j}}\!:\bigg\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,j}L_{T}^{2}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}\leq\eta\Bigg\}.

(6.15)

Then, it follows from
Lemma
5.1

that
there exists
N_{2}=N_{2}(\eta,\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\big((\Omega^{\prime}_{3,N}(\eta))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{6}

(6.16)

for any
N\geq N_{2}
.
In the following, we work on

\Omega^{\prime}_{N}(K,\eta_{0},\eta)
defined by

\displaystyle\Omega_{N}^{\prime}(K,\eta_{0},\eta)=\Omega_{1,N}^{\prime}(\eta_{0})\cap\Omega_{2,N}^{\prime}(K)\cap\Omega_{3,N}^{\prime}(\eta).

(6.17)

Given small
\delta>0
,
by choosing

K=K(\delta)
sufficiently large,
it follows from
(
6.17
)
with (
6.12
), (
6.14
), and (
6.16
)
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big((\Omega_{N}^{\prime}(K,\eta_{0},\eta))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{2}

(6.18)

for any
N\geq\max(N_{1},N_{2})
,
where
N_{1}(\eta_{0},\delta)

and
N_{2}(\eta,\delta)

are as in (
6.12
)
and (
6.16
), respectively.

We now consider (
6.9
)
on an interval
J=[t_{0},t_{1}]\subset[0,T]
with
|J|\leq 1
,
starting at time
t_{0}
.
By applying a slight modification of
(
3.6
)-(
3.12
),
where
\boldsymbol{Z}_{N}=\big\{Z_{j}^{(1)},Z_{k,j}^{(2)},Z_{k,j}^{(3)}\big\}_{j,k=1}^{N}

are as in (
2.20
),
with (
6.13
) and (
6.15
)
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,j}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}&\leq C_{1}N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,j}(t_{0})\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}\\
&\quad+C_{2}|J|\Big(\|\vec{v}_{N,k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}^{2}+K^{2}\Big)N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,j}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}\\
&\quad+C_{3}|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta,\end{split}

(6.19)

where
the
X^{s}_{N}(J)
-norm is as in
(
5.46
).
Suppose that

\displaystyle\|\vec{v}_{N,j}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}\leq 1

for some interval
J\subset[0,T]
.
Then,
it follows from (
6.19
) that
there exists
\tau=\tau(K(\delta))=\tau(\delta)\in(0,1]
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,j}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}&\leq 2C_{1}N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,j}(t_{0})\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{x}}+2C_{3}\eta,\end{split}

(6.20)

provided that
|J|\leq\tau
.
Compare (
6.20
) with (
5.61
).
Then,
by noting that
\tau
depends only on
\delta
,
and choosing

\eta_{0}=\eta_{0}(\delta)
and
\eta=\eta(\delta)
sufficiently small,
the desired convergence-in-probability (
6.10
)
follows from (
6.11
) and
a slight modification of the iterative argument
in Step 1
of the proof of Theorem
1.4
(i) presented in Subsection
5.2
.
We omit details.

\bullet

Step 2:

In the following, we briefly discuss
how to obtain
the following convergence
in probability
for each
fixed

j\in\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,j}\|_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}_{x}^{s}}\longrightarrow 0

(6.21)

as
N\to\infty
.

We first go over the construction of data sets indexed by
N\gg 1
.
Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Given small
\eta_{0}>0
, define the set
\Omega^{\prime}_{4,j,N}(\eta_{0})\subset\Omega^{\prime}

by setting

\displaystyle\Omega^{\prime}_{4,j,N}(\eta_{0})=\Big\{\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\prime}:N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|v^{(0)}_{N,j}\|_{H_{x}^{s}}\leq\eta_{0}\Big\}.

(6.22)

where
\mathcal{A}_{N}B
is as in (
1.11
).
Then, it follows from Chebyshev’s inequality
and (
6.2
) with (
6.3
) that
there exists
N_{3}=N_{3}(j,\eta_{0},\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\big((\Omega^{\prime}_{4,j,N}(\eta_{0}))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{8}

(6.23)

for any
N\geq N_{3}
.
Given small
\eta>0
,
define the
\Omega^{\prime}_{5,N}(\eta)\subset\Omega^{\prime}

by setting

\displaystyle\Omega^{\prime}_{5,N}(\eta)=\Big\{\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\prime}:N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}\leq\eta\Big\}.

(6.24)

Then, it follows
from the convergence-in-probability (
6.10
)
established in
Step 1 that, given
\delta>0
,
there exists
N_{4}=N_{4}(\eta,\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big((\Omega^{\prime}_{5,N}(\eta))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{8}

(6.25)

for any
N\geq N_{4}
.
Given
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
K\geq 1
, define the set
\Omega^{\prime}_{6,j,N}(K)\subset\Omega^{\prime}
by setting

\displaystyle\Omega^{\prime}_{6,j,N}(K)=\Big\{\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\prime}:\|:\!{\Phi_{k}\Phi_{j}}\!:\|_{\mathcal{A}_{N,k}C_{T}W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}_{x}}\leq K\Big\}.

(6.26)

Then, it follows from Lemma
2.5
(i)
(for the uniform (in
j,k
) moment bound
on the
C_{T}W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}_{x}
-norm of
:\!{\Phi_{k}\Phi_{j}}\!:
)
and Chebyshev’s inequality
that
there exists
K=K(\delta)\gg 1
, independent of
j,N\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big((\Omega^{\prime}_{6,j,N}(K))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{8}.

(6.27)

Lastly,
given small
\eta>0
, define
the set
\Omega^{\prime}_{7,j,N}(\eta)\subset\Omega^{\prime}

by setting

\displaystyle\Omega^{\prime}_{7,j,N}(\eta)\subset\Omega^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Bigg\{\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\prime}:N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}:\!{\Phi_{k}^{2}\Phi_{j}}\!:\bigg\|_{L_{T}^{2}W_{x}^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}\leq\eta\Bigg\}.

(6.28)

Then, it follows from
Lemma
5.1

that
there exists
N_{5}=N_{5}(j,\eta,\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\big((\Omega^{\prime}_{7,j,N}(\eta))^{c}\big)<\frac{\delta}{8}

(6.29)

for any
N\geq N_{5}
.
In the following, we work on

\Omega^{\prime}_{j,N}(K,\eta_{0},\eta)
defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega_{j,N}^{\prime}(K,\eta_{0},\eta)&=\Omega_{N}^{\prime}(K,\eta_{0},\eta)\cap\Omega_{4,j,N}^{\prime}(\eta_{0})\\
&\quad\cap\Omega_{5,N}^{\prime}(\eta)\cap\Omega_{6,j,N}^{\prime}(K)\cap\Omega_{7,j,N}^{\prime}(\eta)\end{split}

(6.30)

where

\Omega_{N}^{\prime}(K,\eta_{0},\eta)
is as in
(
6.17
).
Given small
\delta>0
,
by choosing

K=K(\delta)
sufficiently large,
it follows from
(
6.30
)
with (
6.18
), (
6.23
),
(
6.25
), (
6.27
),
and (
6.29
),
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big((\Omega_{j,N}^{\prime}(K,\eta_{0},\eta))^{c}\big)<\delta

(6.31)

for any
N\geq\max_{\ell=1,\dots,5}N_{\ell}
,
where
N_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,\dots,5
,
are as in
(
6.12
),
(
6.16
),
(
6.23
),
(
6.25
),
and (
6.29
), respectively.

For fixed
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
we now consider (
6.9
)
on an interval
J=[t_{0},t_{1}]\subset[0,T]
with
|J|\leq 1
,
starting at time
t_{0}
.
By applying a slight modification of
(
3.6
)-(
3.12
)
(without taking the
\ell^{2}
-average in
j
;
see also the estimates
in Step 2
of the proof of Theorem
1.4
(i) presented in Subsection
5.2
)
with (
6.13
), (
6.24
),
(
6.26
),
and (
6.28
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,j}\|_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}_{x}^{s}}&\leq C_{1}N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,j}(t_{0})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\\
&\quad+C_{2}|J|\Big(\|\vec{v}_{N,k}\|_{X^{s}_{N}(J)}^{2}+K^{2}\Big)N^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\|\vec{v}_{N,j}\|_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}_{x}^{s}}\\
&\quad+C_{3}|J|^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta(1+K).\end{split}

(6.32)

Compare (
6.32
) with (
5.87
).
Then,
in view of (
6.32
) with (
6.22
) and (
6.31
),
the desired convergence-in-probability (
6.21
)
follows from
a slight modification of the iterative argument
in Step 1
of the proof of Theorem
1.4
(i) presented in Subsection
5.2
.
We omit details.

This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.6
(iii).
